\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0)=R^{3}\left(\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{1}{R-r}\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left({N_{1}}>0\right)\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{exc},R}\left[1-(1-s)^{N_{1}}\right].

(3.5)

Remark 3.7
.

While we have restricted ourselves to proving distributional convergence of the number of pioneers, the argument we use to prove Corollary
3.6
could be used to prove convergence of the law of the
of the whole branching process under
\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left(\,\cdot\,|{N_{1}}>0\right)
as
r\uparrow R
.
This would define an excursion version of the branching Brownian motion. This will not be needed in the rest of this article, and is probably well known in the folklore of the literature on this subject, although we could not find a reference.

Proof of Corollary
3.6
.

Lemmas
2.1
and
3.5
show that for all
s\in(s_{c}(R),1]
,
\mathsf{v}_{s}
is differentiable at
R
. This derivative has the following probabilistic interpretation:

\displaystyle\mathsf{v}_{s}^{\prime}(R)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{\mathsf{v}_{s}(R)-\mathsf{v}_{s}(r)}{R-r}=-\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{1}{R-r}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[1-(1-s)^{N_{1}}\right]

(3.6)

\displaystyle=-\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{1}{R-r}\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left({N_{1}}>0\right)\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[1-(1-s)^{N_{1}}|{N_{1}}>0\right].

When
s=1
, the last expectation on the right hand side is equal to 1 showing that the first limit in (
3.4
) exists, is finite, and is nonzero since
\mathsf{v}_{1}^{\prime}(R)\neq 0
by Lemma
3.5
.
The convergence of the second limit in (
3.4
) and the expression (
3.5
) for
\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0)
then follows from this together with (
3.6
) and the identity
\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0)=-R^{3}\mathsf{v}_{s}^{\prime}(R)
, which follows from the same calculation as (
3.3
).

The only item remaining to check is that if
s<s_{c}(R)
then
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{exc},R}\left[(1-s)^{N_{1}}\right]=+\infty
.
Let
k\geq 1
and suppose
r>R/2
.
The probability that a single particle starting on
\partial B(r)
creates at least
k
pioneers on the unit sphere is at least the probability that this particle creates at least one pioneer on
\partial B(R/2)
multiplied by the probability that a single particle starting on
\partial B(R/2)
creates at least
k
pioneers on the unit sphere. Hence, for all
k\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{exc},R}\left({N_{1}}\geq k\right)=\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left({N_{1}}\geq k\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left({N_{1}}>0\right)}\geq\left(\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left(N_{R/2}>0\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left(N_{1}>0\right)}\right)\mathbb{P}_{R/2,R}\left({N_{1}}\geq k\right).

Reaching
\partial B(R/2)
is easier than reaching
\partial B(1)
, so the ratio on the right hand side is at least 1. This proves that

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{exc},R}\left({N_{1}}\geq k\right)\geq\mathbb{P}_{R/2,R}\left({N_{1}}\geq k\right)
for every
k\geq 1
and hence that the expectation of
(1-s)^{N_{1}}
is also infinite under the excursion measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{exc},R}
whenever
s<s_{c}(R)
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary
3.6
shows in particular that
-\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0)
is well-defined and finite, as claimed in Proposition
3.3
. We may thus define
\lambda_{R}(s)
(which appears in the statement of that proposition) as
\lambda_{R}(s)=-\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0).
Furthermore, using the expression (
3.5
), we see immediately that
s\mapsto\lambda_{R}(s)
is monotone and continuous (and indeed analytic) over
(s_{c}(R),0]
.

To complete the proof of Proposition
3.3
, it remains to prove the lower bound on the critical value
\lambda_{c}(R)
. (This is the most difficult part of the proposition to prove.) We now state two lemmas, proven in Sections
3.2
and
3.4
respectively, and show how they imply Proposition
3.3
.

The first of these lemmas, Lemma
3.8
, gives an alternative description of
\lambda_{c}(R)
from a purely analytical point of view. This is a powerful tool to understand the critical point
\lambda_{c}(R)
, which was previously defined only implicitly as the maximal slope at the origin that can be obtained from our probabilistic solutions. This lemma will be proved in Section
3.2
using Pringsheim’s theorem as a key input.

Lemma 3.8

(Analytic description of
\lambda_{c}
)
.

Recall that
R=e^{L}
. The equality

\lambda_{c}(R)=\sup\left\{\lambda_{2}>0:\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda_{2})\mapsto g_{\lambda_{1}}(L)\text{ is analytic and }\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{1}}g_{\lambda_{1}}(L)<0\text{ for every }\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda_{2})\right\}.

holds for every
R=e^{L}>1
.

The second lemma establishes various properties of the map
(\lambda,x)\mapsto g_{\lambda}(x)
, most notably that
g_{\lambda}(x)
is monotone in
\lambda
when
\lambda
is sufficiently small. (The numerical solutions plotted in Figure
3.1
suggest that this is
not
true for larger values of
\lambda
.) The proof of this result is contained in Section
3.4
, and is the most involved part of the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Figure 3.1
:
Plots of numerical approximations of
g_{\lambda}(x)
for different positive values of
\lambda
. Left: If
\lambda
is small enough (e.g.
\lambda<1
),
g_{\lambda}
possesses a probabilistic representation (Theorem
3.1
), and the curves for different small values of
\lambda
cannot cross each other. Right: The numerical solutions plotted here suggest that this non-intersection property fails for larger values of
\lambda
, even when the solutions are non-positive. Further numerics suggest that the critical value for solutions to be monotonic in
\lambda
is around
2.4
, see Figure
3.3
.

Figure 3.2
:

Left: Numerical plots of
u_{\lambda}(t):=\lambda^{-1}g_{\lambda}(\lambda^{-1}t)
for various large values of
\lambda
. These numerical computations suggest that
g_{\lambda}

can
become positive when
\lambda
is large. The solutions with
\lambda=150
and
\lambda=500
cross the
x
-axis outside of the plot. Further numerics suggest that the critical value of
\lambda
for
g_{\lambda}
to remain non-positive is roughly
11.2

Lemma 3.9

(Properties of
(\lambda,x)\mapsto g_{\lambda}(x)
)
.

1.

For each
\lambda\in[0,2)
, the function
g_{\lambda}
, which was defined as the maximal solution to the initial value problem (
3.1
), is defined and non-positive on
[0,\infty)
.

2.

The map
(\lambda,x)\in[0,2)\times[0,\infty)\mapsto g_{\lambda}(x)
is real-analytic (viewed as a function of two real variables).

3.

If
\lambda\in[0,1)
then
\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}g_{\lambda}(x)<0
for every
x\geq 0
.

We now briefly explain how Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
imply Proposition
3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
given Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
.

As already mentioned, by Corollary
3.6
, the function
\mathsf{g}_{s}
is differentiable at
0
for every
s\in(s_{c}(R),0]
. The expression (
3.5
) for
\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0)
in terms of
s
shows that
\lambda_{R}
is continuous and decreasing. Finally, combining Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
, we obtain that
\lambda_{c}(R)\geq 1
.
∎

3.2
Analytic description of
\lambda_{c}
: Proof of Lemma
3.8

In this section we prove Lemma
3.8
. We define

\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R):=\sup\left\{\lambda_{2}>0:\lambda_{1}\in[0,\lambda_{2}]\mapsto g_{\lambda_{1}}(L)\text{ is analytic and }\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{1}}g_{\lambda_{1}}(L)<0\text{ for every }\lambda_{1}\in[0,\lambda_{2}]\right\}

and aim to show that
\lambda_{c}(R)=\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
.

Proof of Lemma
3.8
.

We start by showing that
\lambda_{c}(R)\leq\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
.
By Corollary
3.6
(specifically (
3.5
)), the map
\lambda_{R}:s\in(s_{c}(R),0]\mapsto-\mathsf{g}_{s}^{\prime}(0)\in[0,\lambda_{c}(R))
is analytic (since it is the Laplace transform of a random variable). Moreover, its derivative

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\lambda_{R}(s)=-R^{3}\left(\lim_{r\uparrow R}\frac{1}{R-r}\mathbb{P}_{r,R}\left({N_{1}}>0\right)\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{exc},R}\left[{N_{1}}(1-s)^{{N_{1}}-1}\right]

is negative on
(s_{c}(R),0]
since
{N_{1}}\geq 1
almost surely under the measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{exc},R}
. It follows that the inverse function

\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{c}(R))\mapsto g_{\lambda}(L)\in(s_{c}(R),0]

is also analytic and has negative derivative. This implies that
\lambda_{c}(R)\leq\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
.

We now apply Pringsheim’s theorem to prove the reverse inequality. Assume for contradiction that there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\lambda_{c}(R)+\varepsilon<\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
. By definition of
\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
, this means that the map
f:\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{c}(R)+\varepsilon]\mapsto g_{\lambda}(L)
is analytic and has negative derivative. Let
[s_{c}(R)-\delta,0]
be the image of
[0,\lambda_{c}(R)+\varepsilon]
under
f
. Since the derivative of
f
is negative,
\delta
is positive. Moreover, the inverse function
f^{-1}:[s_{c}(R)-\delta,0]\to[0,\lambda_{c}(R)+\varepsilon]
is analytic and agrees with
\lambda_{R}
on
(s_{c}(R),0]
. The contradiction comes from the fact that
s_{c}(R)
is a singularity of
\lambda_{R}
, meaning that
\lambda_{R}
cannot be analytically extended beyond
s_{c}(R)
. Indeed,
\lambda_{R}
is a power series in
-s
with non negative coefficients (see (
3.5
)) and
|s_{c}(R)|
is its radius of convergence. By Pringsheim’s theorem,
s_{c}(R)
is therefore a singularity of
\lambda_{R}
.
This shows that
\lambda_{c}(R)+\varepsilon
cannot be smaller than
\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
which concludes the proof of the reverse inequality
\lambda_{c}(R)\geq\tilde{\lambda}_{c}(R)
.
∎

Figure 3.3
:
Numerical plots of
-\frac{x}{2}g_{\lambda}(x)
with
x=1000
as a function of
\lambda
. Left: The asymptotics
g_{\lambda}(x)\sim-2/x
as
x\to\infty
, proven for fixed
\lambda\in(0,1)
in Section
5
, is verified numerically for a larger range of
\lambda
values. The convergence appears to hold uniformly away from
0
and some critical value around
11.25
. Right: Zooming in on this plot near its maximum. The maximum of the function is attained numerically at
\lambda\approx 2.43
, suggesting that the critical value for our probabilistic representation of solutions to hold is around
\inf_{R}\lambda_{c}(R)\approx\lambda_{c}(e^{1000})\approx 2.43
.

3.3
A Grönwall-type lemma

Before moving to the proof of Lemma
3.9
, we state and prove an elementary Grönwall-type lemma which will be used in several places in this paper (including the proof of Lemma
3.9
). Taking
u=0
in the first case yields the standard form of Grönwall’s lemma.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
a<b
, and suppose that we are given two functions
u:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
and
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
, where
u\in L^{1}([a,b])
and
f
is differentiable.

1.

If there exists a constant
c
such that

f^{\prime}(x)+cf(x)\leq u(x)\qquad\text{ for every }x\in(a,b)

(3.7)

then

e^{cy}f(y)-e^{cx}f(x)\leq\int_{x}^{y}e^{ct}u(t)\mathrm{d}t

for every
x,y\in[a,b]
with
x<y
.

2.

If there exists a constant
c
such that

xf^{\prime}(x)+cf(x)\leq u(x)\qquad\text{ for every }x\in(a,b)

(3.8)

then

y^{c}f(y)-x^{c}f(x)\leq\int_{x}^{y}t^{c-1}u(t)\mathrm{d}t

for every
x,y\in[a,b]
with
x<y
.

We can reverse the signs of both inequalities in both cases.

Proof.

First suppose that the inequality (
3.7
) holds. Multiplying the inequality by
e^{cx}
leads to the differential inequality

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(e^{cx}f(x))\leq u(x)e^{cx}

for every

x\in(a,b)
, which can then be integrated to reach the desired conclusion.
Now suppose that the inequality (
3.8
) holds. In this case we multiply by
x^{c}
to get that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}(x^{c}f(x))\leq x^{c-1}u(x)
for every
x\in(a,b)
, from which we may conclude as before. Both proofs work identically if the directions of all inequalities are reversed.
∎

3.4
Proof of Lemma
3.9

In this section we prove Lemma
3.9
, thereby concluding the proofs of Proposition
3.3
and Theorem
3.1
.

We begin by observing that if
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are solutions to the ODE
g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2}
, then the difference
f=g_{2}-g_{1}
satisfies the ODE

f^{\prime\prime}+2f^{\prime}+\varepsilon f=0\qquad\text{ where }\qquad\varepsilon=-g_{1}-g_{2}.

The following result studies properties of solutions to such equations that hold whenever
\varepsilon
is non-negative and bounded away from
1
.
This will be useful when we will want to show that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}g_{\lambda}(x)<0
for all
x>0
as stated in Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\varepsilon:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be a continuous nonnegative function with
\eta:=\sup_{[0,\infty)}\varepsilon<1
. The maximal solutions to the ODE
f^{\prime\prime}+2f^{\prime}+\varepsilon f=0
with
f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(0)<0
are defined on
[0,\infty)
and satisfy
f(x)<0
for all
x>0
. More quantitatively,
the bound

f(x)\leq\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{2\sqrt{1-\eta}}\left(e^{-(1-\sqrt{1-\eta})x}-e^{-(1+\sqrt{1-\eta})x}\right)

(3.9)

holds for every
x\geq 0
.

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

Since the ODE
f^{\prime\prime}+2f^{\prime}+\varepsilon f=0
is linear, the fact that its maximal solutions
are defined on
[0,\infty)
is standard and requires only that
\varepsilon
is continuous (or bounded). We focus on showing that if
f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(0)<0
then
f
stays nonpositive.
Let
c_{\pm}=1\pm\sqrt{1-\eta}
, which are real since
\eta<1
.
Let
x_{*}=\inf\{x>0:f(x)=0\}
(with
\inf\emptyset=+\infty
as usual). We are going to show that
x_{*}=+\infty
.
On
[0,x_{*})
, we have
f^{\prime\prime}+2f^{\prime}+\eta f\leq 0.
The constants
c_{+}
and
c_{-}
have been defined so that
c_{+}+c_{-}=2
and
c_{+}c_{-}=\eta
, allowing us to write

\displaystyle(f^{\prime}+c_{+}f)^{\prime}+c_{-}(f^{\prime}+c_{+}f)=f^{\prime\prime}+2f^{\prime}+\eta f\leq 0

for every
x\in[0,x_{*})
.
By Lemma
3.10
, this leads to
f^{\prime}(x)+c_{+}f(x)\leq f^{\prime}(0)e^{-c_{-}x}
for all
x<x_{*}
. Applying Lemma
3.10
once more, we get that

f(x)\leq\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{2\sqrt{1-\eta}}\left(e^{-(1-\sqrt{1-\eta})x}-e^{-(1+\sqrt{1-\eta})x}\right)\quad\text{for every $x\in[0,x_{*})$}.

Since
f^{\prime}(0)<0
, the function on the right hand side stays (strictly) negative on
(0,\infty)
showing that
x_{*}=+\infty
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We will also need the following intermediate result in order to prove Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.12
.

If
\lambda\in[0,2)
, then
g_{\lambda}
is defined on
[0,\infty)
, is negative on
(0,\infty)
, and satisfies

\lim_{x\to\infty}g_{\lambda}(x)=\lim_{x\to\infty}g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)=0,

(3.10)

uniformly on compact subsets of
\{\lambda\in[0,2)\}
.
Moreover,
\sup_{[0,\infty)}|g_{\lambda}|\leq\lambda/2
and
\sup_{[0,\infty)}|g_{\lambda}^{\prime}|\leq 2\lambda
for every
\lambda\in[0,2)
.

Remark 3.13
.

The proof of this lemma also shows that if
\lambda\in(0,2)
then
g_{\lambda}
is unimodal, i.e., is decreasing on some interval
[0,x_{0}]
and increasing on the complementary interval
[x_{0},\infty)
for some
x_{0}=x_{0}(\lambda)>0
; we give a formal statement of this in Lemma
4.2
.

Proof of Lemma
3.12
.

Let
\lambda\in[0,2)
and let
I
be the maximal subinterval of
[0,\infty)
containing
0
on which
g_{\lambda}
is defined. Integrating the inequality
g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}\geq 0
(using Lemma
3.10
) leads to

g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)\geq-\lambda e^{-2x}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{\lambda}(x)\geq-\frac{\lambda}{2}(1-e^{-2x})\qquad\text{ for every }x\in I.

(3.11)

As in the proof of Lemma
3.11
, we set
x_{*}:=\inf\{x\in I:g_{\lambda}(x)\geq 0\}
and will argue that
x_{*}=\sup I=\infty
. For each
x\in[0,x_{*})
we have by (
3.11
) that
-\lambda/2\leq g_{\lambda}\leq 0
and hence that

g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}=g_{\lambda}^{2}\leq-\lambda g_{\lambda}/2\qquad\text{ for every $x\in[0,x_{*})$.}

Using that
\lambda/2<1
and
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=-\lambda
and arguing as in the proof of Lemma
3.11
, this implies that

g_{\lambda}(x)\leq-\frac{\lambda}{2\sqrt{1-\lambda/2}}\left(e^{-(1-\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})x}-e^{-(1+\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})x}\right)

(3.12)

for every
x<x_{*}
, while the inequality written in the proof of Lemma
3.11
as
f^{\prime}(x)+c_{+}f(x)\leq f^{\prime}(0)e^{-c_{-}x}
applied with
f=g_{\lambda}
and
\eta=\lambda/2
yields that

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\leq-\lambda e^{-(1-\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})x}-(1+\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})g_{\lambda}(x)

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq-\lambda e^{-(1-\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})x}+(1+\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})\frac{\lambda}{2}(1-e^{-2x}).

The inequality
(
3.12
) implies that
x_{*}=\sup I
. Together, these estimates prove that
g_{\lambda}
and
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}
are uniformly bounded on
I
, which implies by standard ODE theory that
g_{\lambda}
is defined on
[0,\infty)
.
The fact that
\sup|g_{\lambda}|\leq\lambda/2
and
\sup|g_{\lambda}^{\prime}|\leq 2\lambda
follows from (
3.11
), (
3.12
), and (
3.13
) using the inequality

\lambda+\frac{1}{2}(1+\sqrt{1-\lambda/2})\lambda\leq 2\lambda
.

It only remains to check that
g_{\lambda}(x)\to 0
and
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\{\lambda\in[0,2)\}
.
First note that using the first line of
(
3.11
), we find
\limsup g^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)\leq 2\limsup|g_{\lambda}(x)|=0
. Together with the lower bound in (
3.12
), this shows that the uniform convergence of
g_{\lambda}(x)
to 0 implies that of
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)
to 0. We therefore focus on
g_{\lambda}
. Let
\lambda_{*}\in(0,2)
and let
\varepsilon>0
be small.
We define the following quantities:

•

x_{0}=\inf\{x>0:g^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)=0\}

•

x_{\varepsilon}=\inf\{x>0:\sup_{[x,\infty)}|g_{\lambda}|\leq\varepsilon\}

•

y_{\varepsilon}=\inf\{x>x_{0}:g_{\lambda}(x)\geq-\varepsilon
}.

We want to show that for any fixed
\varepsilon>0
,
x_{\varepsilon}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{*}]
. If
\lambda\in[0,2\varepsilon]
, then
\sup|g_{\lambda}|\leq\lambda/2\leq\varepsilon
and
x_{*}=0
. Take now
\lambda\in[2\varepsilon,\lambda_{*}]
. Let us first show that
x_{0}
is bounded uniformly. If
x_{0}\leq 1
there is nothing to show.
If
x_{0}
is larger than 1, then for all
x\in[1,x_{0}]
,
g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(x)+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)=g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}\geq g_{\lambda}(1)^{2}
. Integrating this differential inequality (Lemma
3.10
) gives that
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}g_{\lambda}(1)^{2}(1-e^{2-2x})+g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(1)e^{2-2x}
for every
x\in[1,x_{0})
. In particular, this shows that
x_{0}
is finite. Because
|g_{\lambda}(1)|
is bounded away from zero uniformly in
\lambda\in[2\varepsilon,\lambda_{*}]
(which follows from (
3.12
)) and because
|g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(1)|\leq 2\lambda
is uniformly bounded away from infinity, the bound we obtain on
x_{0}
is uniform in
\lambda\in[2\varepsilon,\lambda_{*}]
.

Next, integrating the inequality
g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}>0
between
x_{0}
and
x>x_{0}
(Lemma
3.10
) yields that
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)>0
for all
x>x_{0}
.
Therefore, for all
x\geq y_{\varepsilon}
we have
g_{\lambda}(x)\geq-\varepsilon
, hence
x_{\varepsilon}\leq y_{\varepsilon}
.
It therefore suffices to show that
y_{\varepsilon}-x_{0}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
to conclude.
Either
y_{\varepsilon}=x_{0}
, or for all
x\in(x_{0},y_{\varepsilon})
,

g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(x)+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)=g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}\geq\varepsilon^{2}
. Integrating this inequality between
x_{0}
and
x\in(x_{0},y_{\varepsilon})
with Lemma
3.10
, we obtain that
g^{\prime}(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}(1-e^{2(x_{0}-y_{\varepsilon})})
. Integrating further yields
g(y_{\varepsilon})-g(x_{0})\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2}(y_{\varepsilon}-x_{0})-\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon^{4}(1-e^{2(x_{0}-y_{\varepsilon}])})
. This provides the desired uniform bound on
y_{\varepsilon}-x_{0}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

We have already shown that
g_{\lambda}
is well-defined and non-positive on
[0,\infty)
when
\lambda\in[0,2)
.
We next show that
(\lambda,x)\in[0,2)\times[0,\infty)\mapsto g_{\lambda}(x)
is analytic. In fact, we will show the stronger statement (
3.14
) below.
For
x_{0}\leq 0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, let us denote by
g_{x_{0},\mu}
the unique maximal solution to the Cauchy problem
g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2},g(0)=x_{0}
and
g^{\prime}(0)=\mu
. Fix
\lambda_{*}\in(0,2)
and let

\text{Data}=\{(g_{\lambda}(x),g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)):\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{*}],x\in[0,\infty)\}

be the set of possible initial conditions (i.e., points in phase space) that are visited by the set of solutions to our original initial value problem with
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{*}]
.
Lemma
3.12
(specifically the uniform convergence (
3.10
)) implies that this set is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We claim that the function

(x_{0},\mu,x)\in\mathrm{Data}\times[0,\infty)\mapsto(g_{x_{0},\mu}(x),g_{x_{0},\mu}^{\prime}(x))\in(-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{R}\quad\text{is~analytic.}

(3.14)

The analyticity of
(\lambda,x)\in[0,2)\times[0,\infty)\mapsto g_{\lambda}(x)
will be a direct consequence of (
3.14
) since
\lambda_{*}
can be as close to 2 as desired.
We now explain the proof of this claim. This will be a quick consequence of the fact that the ODE is autonomous and that the set
Data
is compact (it is likely that the detailed derivation below is standard).
Indeed, by compactness of the set Data and the Cauchy–Kovalevskaya theorem, there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for all
(x_{0},\mu)\in\text{Data}
,

(x_{0},\mu,x)\in\text{Data}\times[0,\varepsilon]\mapsto(g_{x_{0},\mu}(x),g_{x_{0},\mu}^{\prime}(x))\quad\text{is~analytic.}

(3.15)

For all
(x_{0},\mu)\in\text{Data}
and
x\in[\varepsilon/2,3\varepsilon/2]
, we have

\left(g_{x_{0},\mu}(x),g_{x_{0},\mu}^{\prime}(x)\right)=\left(g_{\hat{x}_{0},\hat{\mu}}(x-\varepsilon/2),g_{\hat{x}_{0},\hat{\mu}}^{\prime}(x-\varepsilon/2)\right)\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{x}_{0}=g_{x_{0},\mu}(\varepsilon/2),\hat{\mu}=g^{\prime}_{x_{0},\mu}(\varepsilon/2).

By (
3.15
),
\hat{x}_{0}
and
\hat{\mu}
are analytic functions of
x_{0}
and
\mu
. Also, by definition of
Data
,
(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{\mu})\in\text{Data}
. So again by (
3.15
)
\left(g_{\hat{x}_{0},\hat{\mu}}(x-\varepsilon/2),g_{\hat{x}_{0},\hat{\mu}}^{\prime}(x-\varepsilon/2)\right)
are analytic functions of
\hat{x}_{0}
,
\hat{\mu}
and
x\in[\varepsilon/2,3\varepsilon/2]
. Overall, we have written

(x_{0},\mu,x)\in\text{Data}\times[\varepsilon/2,3\varepsilon/2]\mapsto(g_{x_{0},\mu}(x),g_{x_{0},\mu}^{\prime}(x))

as the composition of analytic functions. Putting things together, we have shown that the above map is analytic on
\text{Data}\times[0,3\varepsilon/2]
. We can iterate this procedure to obtain analycity on
\text{Data}\times[0,\infty)
as claimed in (
3.14
).

To conclude the proof, it remains to show that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}g_{\lambda}(x)<0
for every
x\geq 0
and
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Let
0<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\lambda_{*}<1
.
We observe that the function
f=g_{\lambda_{2}}-g_{\lambda_{1}}
satisfies the ODE

f^{\prime\prime}+2f^{\prime}=-\varepsilon f
,
f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(0)=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}<0

with
\varepsilon=-(g_{\lambda_{1}}+g_{\lambda_{2}})
. By Lemma
3.12
,
\varepsilon
is nonnegative and
\sup_{[0,\infty)}\varepsilon\leq(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2})/2<\lambda_{*}
which is smaller than 1. Hence, by Lemma
3.11
, the function
f
is negative on
(0,\infty)
and satisfies the quantitative bound

g_{\lambda_{2}}(x)-g_{\lambda_{1}}(x)\leq-\frac{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}}{2\sqrt{1-\lambda_{*}}}\left(e^{-(1-\sqrt{1-\lambda_{*}})x}-e^{-(1+\sqrt{1-\lambda_{*}})x}\right)

for every
x\geq 0
, which implies that
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}g_{\lambda}(x)<0
for all
x\geq 0
and
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{*})
. This concludes the proof since
\lambda_{*}
can be as close to 1 as desired.
∎

4
Tail estimates with killing I: Upper bounds

In this section, we initiate the study of the long time behaviour of solutions to
g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2}
. Together with Theorem
3.1
, this will be already enough to prove the upper bounds of Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
.
We prove relevant estimates on solutions to the ODE in Section
4.2
then deduce the desired upper bounds on the tail of the number of pioneers in Section
4.3
. We start in Section
4.1
by recording for ease of future reference a lemma concerning the leading order term of the probability of hitting a small ball.

4.1
Preliminary estimates on the hitting probability

In Lemma
4.1
below, we give preliminary estimates on the hitting probability both in finite and infinite volumes. These will be useful throughout the paper. Later, we will provide much sharper asymptotics in the infinite volume case; see Theorems
1.7
and
5.1
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
x_{0}>0
. For each
\delta>0
, there exists
R_{0}>0
and
x_{*}>x_{0}
depending only on
x_{0}
and
\delta
such that for all
R\geq R_{0}
and
r\in(0,e^{-x^{*}}R]
,

\displaystyle\text{Without killing:}\quad e^{-\delta}\frac{2}{(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}\log(R/r)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,\infty}\left(N_{r}>0\right)\leq e^{\delta}\frac{2}{(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}\log(R/r)},\quad\text{and}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\text{With killing:}\hskip 51.0pte^{-\delta}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}\log(R/r)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)\leq e^{\delta}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}\log(R/r)}.

(4.2)

Proof.

The estimate (
4.1
) is folklore. It follows also directly from Lemma
5.11
. We omit the details. We now explain how the estimate (
4.2
) is obtained.
Consider an intermediate scale
r^{\prime}\in(r,e^{-x_{0}}R)
. Starting from
\partial B(e^{-x_{0}}R)
and conditionally on
N_{r^{\prime}}
, the ball
B(r)
can be reached if, and only if, at least one pioneer on
\partial B(r^{\prime})
has had a progeny that reached
B(r)
. To proceed, we will choose the intermediate scale
r^{\prime}
in such a way that the following two properties will hold:

1.

r^{\prime}
will be sufficiently large so that, conditional on the event that
B(r)
is hit, there will with high probability be exactly one pioneer on
\partial B(r^{\prime})
whose descendents hit
B(r)
. This will mean that the probability
B(r)
is hit starting from
e^{-x_{0}}R
is asymptotic to the expected number of pioneers on
\partial B(r^{\prime})
multiplied by the probability one of these particles hits
B(r)
.

2.

r^{\prime}
will be sufficiently small that the hitting probability of
B(r)
with and without killing will be comparable when starting from
\partial B(r^{\prime})
.

Once such an intermediate scale is found, we will be able to compute the desired finite-volume hitting probability to leading order by multiplying the expected number of pioneers on
\partial B(r^{\prime})
(which is just a Green’s function computation) with the infinite-volume hitting probability from
\partial B(r^{\prime})
, which is estimated in (
4.1
).

First of all, arguing as in Corollary
2.9
, there exists
C>0
such that for all starting point
y\in B(e^{-x_{0}}R)
,

\mathbb{P}_{y,\infty}\left(N_{R}>0\right)\leq\frac{C}{R^{2}}.

(4.3)

We now introduce some parameters and the relevant scales.
Let
\delta>0
. Let
x_{*}>x_{0}
be large enough so that for all
x\geq x_{*}
,

\delta x>x_{0},\quad 1-e^{-2\delta x}\geq e^{-\delta}\quad\text{and}\quad e^{2\delta x}\geq C\delta^{-1}x,

(4.4)

where
C
is the constant appearing in (
4.3
).
Let
x\geq x_{*}
,
\xi=\delta x
and
R\geq 1
. Denote by
r=e^{-x}R
the target scale and
r^{\prime}=e^{-\xi}R
the intermediate scale.
By (
4.1
), we may further assume that
x_{*}
is large enough so that

e^{-\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(r^{\prime}/r)}\leq\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},\infty}\left(N_{r}>0\right)\leq e^{\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(r^{\prime}/r)}.

(4.5)

As already mentioned, the ball
B(r)
can be reached if, and only if, at least one pioneer on
\partial B(r^{\prime})
has a descendent that reaches
B(r)
. Denoting
p=\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)
, we thus have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)=\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[1-(1-p)^{N_{r^{\prime}}}\right].

We bound
np-\frac{1}{2}n(n-1)p^{2}\leq 1-(1-p)^{n}\leq np
to get that

p\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}p^{2}\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}(N_{r^{\prime}}-1)\right]\leq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)\leq p\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}\right].

(4.6)

To conclude, we need to estimate the first and second moment of
N_{r^{\prime}}
and the probability
p
.
In the remainder of the proof, we will write
e^{\pm\delta}
for a quantity that is bounded between
e^{-\delta}
and
e^{\delta}
, the precise value of which may vary from line to line.

The first moment of
N_{r^{\prime}}
is explicit and equal to the probability that a Brownian path starting on the sphere
\partial B(e^{-x_{0}}R,R)
hits
B(r^{\prime})
before reaching
\partial B(R)
:

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}\right]=\frac{e^{2x_{0}}-1}{R^{2}/(r^{\prime})^{2}-1}=e^{\pm\delta}\frac{e^{2x_{0}}-1}{R^{2}}(r^{\prime})^{2},

where we used the second inequality in (
4.4
) in the second equality. Following the same computation as in
[
AS22
, Proof of Lemma 3.5]
, the second moment is bounded by

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,\infty}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{C}{R^{2}}(r^{\prime})^{4}\log\frac{R}{r^{\prime}}.

We next prove upper and lower bounds on the probability
p=\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)
. For the upper bound, we can simply ignore finite-volume effects to write
p\leq\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},\infty}\left(N_{r}>0\right)
, then estimate this infinite-volume probability using (
4.5
). We obtain that

p\leq\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},\infty}\left(N_{r}>0\right)\leq e^{\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(r^{\prime}/r)}\leq e^{3\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(R/r)}

where the last inequality follows from
\log(r^{\prime}/r)=x-\xi=(1-\delta)x=(1-\delta)\log(R/r)\geq e^{-2\delta}\log(R/r)
. For the lower bound, we have by (
4.3
) and (
4.5
),

p\geq\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},\infty}\left(N_{r}>0\right)-\mathbb{P}_{r^{\prime},\infty}\left(N_{R}>0\right)\geq e^{-\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(r^{\prime}/r)}-\frac{C}{R^{2}}.

(4.7)

Using the third inequality in (
4.4
),
we further have

C\frac{(r^{\prime})^{2}}{R^{2}}\log(r^{\prime}/r)=Ce^{-2\xi}(x-\xi)=C(1-\delta)e^{-2\delta x}x\leq\delta,

showing that

p\geq e^{-3\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(r^{\prime}/r)}\geq e^{-3\delta}\frac{2}{(r^{\prime})^{2}\log(R/r)}.

Putting things together, it follows that

p\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}\right]=e^{\pm 4\delta}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}\log(R/r)}\quad\text{and}\quad p^{2}\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{r^{\prime}}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{C}{R^{2}}\frac{\log(R/r^{\prime})}{(\log(r^{\prime}/r))^{2}}\leq\frac{C^{\prime}\delta}{R^{2}\log(R/r)},

and injecting these two estimates in (
4.6
) concludes the proof of (
4.2
).
∎

4.2
Study of
g_{\lambda}
when
\lambda
is small

Recall that the function
g_{\lambda}
was defined in (
3.1
) as the maximal solution to the initial value problem
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}+2g^{\prime}_{\lambda}=g_{\lambda}^{2}
,
g_{\lambda}(0)=0
,
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(0)=-\lambda
, which is defined on the entire interval
[0,\infty)
when
0\leq\lambda<2
by Lemma
3.12
.
Our goal in this section is to establish a precise quantitative understanding of
g_{\lambda}
when
\lambda
is a small positive number.

Our
analysis will be based on a decomposition
3
3

3

These three phases are analogous to what are called the “inner layer”, the “matching region”, and the “outer layer” in fluid dynamics. The interested reader can find many fascinating discussions of associated phenomena by searching for “boundary layer theory”.
of the phase portrait of
g_{\lambda}
into three “phases” as depicted in Figure
4.2
. Before stating precise results, let us first describe these phases intuitively. In the first phase, which can be thought of as the “integrated exponential phase”,
g^{2}_{\lambda}
is negligible compared to
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
and
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}
and the derivative
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}
approximately solves the ODE
(g_{\lambda}^{\prime})^{\prime}=-2(g^{\prime}_{\lambda})
. This phase lasts until
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
reaches
0
. Following the integrated exponential phase,
g_{\lambda}
then stays roughly constant until
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}
reaches
0
. We think of this as the “constant phase”. Finally, after
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}
has reached
0
, it will stay much smaller than
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
and
g^{2}_{\lambda}
. In this final phase,
g_{\lambda}
approximately solves the ODE
2g^{\prime}_{\lambda}=g^{2}_{\lambda}
and
g_{\lambda}(x)
looks very much like
-2/x
; we think of this as the “power law” phase. The power-law phase has a much larger “natural time scale” than the other phases:
if
\lambda
is very small and one graphs the function
g_{\lambda}
on the natural time-scale of the third phase (where
x
is proportional to
1/\lambda
), the first two phases look like a near-instantaneous change in the values of
g_{\lambda}
and
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
, while the third phase looks almost exactly like the rational function
2\lambda/(4\lambda x+4)
. See Figure
4.1
for plots of numerical approximations to
g_{\lambda}
supporting this picture. Precise versions of each of these claims will be proven in the remainder of this section.

Let us now make these claims precise. Consider the parameters
x_{0}=x_{0}(\lambda)
and
x_{1}=x_{1}(\lambda)
defined by

x_{0}:=\inf\{x>0:g^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)=0\}\quad\text{and}\quad x_{1}:=\inf\{x>x_{0}:g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}=0\}.

The three phases discussed above will correspond to the intervals
[0,x_{0}]
,
[x_{0},x_{1}]
, and
[x_{1},\infty)
. These phases also have the following interpretation:

Figure 4.1
:

Left: Numerical plots of
u_{\lambda}(t):=\lambda^{-1}g_{\lambda}(\lambda^{-1}t)
for various values of
0\leq\lambda\leq 2
. The first two phases become near-instantaneous under this scaling as
\lambda\downarrow 0
. Right: Numerical plots of
u_{\lambda}(t):=\lambda^{-1}g_{\lambda}(\lambda^{-1}t)
(blue) and its first three derivatives (yellow, green, and red respectively) with
\lambda=1/8
. The values
t=\lambda x_{0}
and
t=\lambda x_{1}
corresponding to
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
are represented by dashed lines. Here we have zoomed in on smaller values of
x
to give more insight into the first two phases.

Lemma 4.2

(Unimodality)
.

Let
\lambda\in[0,2)
. Then
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
are both finite, the function
g_{\lambda}
is strictly decreasing on
[0,x_{0}]
and strictly increasing on
[x_{0},\infty)
, and the derivative
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}
is strictly increasing on
[0,x_{1}]
and strictly decreasing on
[x_{1},\infty)
.

Proof.

The definition of
x_{0}
ensures that
g_{\lambda}
is strictly decreasing on
[0,x_{0}]
, and since
g_{\lambda}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
by Lemma
3.12
we must have that
x_{0}<\infty
. Meanwhile, using Lemma
3.10
to integrate the inequality
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}>0
between
x>x_{0}
and
x_{0}
yields that
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)>0
for all
x>x_{0}
, so that
g_{\lambda}
is strictly increasing on
[x_{0},\infty)
. We now prove the claim concerning
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
. When
x\in[0,x_{0}]
,
g_{\lambda}
is negative and
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is non-positive, so that
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}=g^{2}_{\lambda}-2g^{\prime}_{\lambda}>0
as desired, while the definition of
x_{1}
ensures that
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is strictly increasing on
[x_{0},x_{1}]
also. Again, this easily implies that
x_{1}
is finite since
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
. Finally, differentiating both sides of the ODE
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}+2g^{\prime}_{\lambda}=g^{2}_{\lambda}
yields that
g^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\lambda}+2g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}=2g_{\lambda}g_{\lambda}^{\prime}
, and since
g_{\lambda}
is negative on
(0,\infty)
and
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is positive on
(x_{0},\infty)
it follows that

g^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(x)+2g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(x)<0
for every
x>x_{0}
. Using Lemma
3.10
to integrate this inequality from
x_{1}
to
x>x_{1}
implies that
g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(x)<0
for every
x>x_{1}
, so that
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
is strictly decreasing on
[x_{1},\infty)
as claimed.
∎

Figure 4.2
:

Schematic representation of the phase portrait of
g
. In blue,
g^{\prime}
against
g
and in red approximations corresponding to different phases. In the first phase,
g^{2}
is negligible compared to
g^{\prime\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
. In the second very short phase,
g
is almost constant (
g^{\prime}
negligible). In the last one,
g^{\prime\prime}
is negligible compared to
g^{\prime}
and
g^{2}
.

We now begin to analyze these phases in more quantitative detail for small values of
\lambda
. Our first result in this direction provides estimates that are useful to understand the first phase
[0,x_{0}]
.

Lemma 4.3

(Phase 1)
.

We have that

x_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{8}{\lambda}+O\left(\lambda\log\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad g_{\lambda}(x_{0})=-\left(1+O\left(\lambda\right)\right)\frac{\lambda}{2}\qquad\text{ as $\lambda\downarrow 0$}

(4.8)

and that

-\frac{\lambda}{2}(1-e^{-2x})\leq g_{\lambda}(x)\leq-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(1-e^{-2x}\right)+\frac{1}{8}\lambda^{2}\left(x-\frac{1-e^{-4x}}{4}+2xe^{-2x}-(1-e^{-2x})\right)

(4.9)

for every
\lambda\in[0,2)
and
x\geq 0
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

To lighten notation we write
g=g_{\lambda}
.
The ODE satisfied by
g
can be rewritten as

(g^{\prime}e^{2x})^{\prime}=g^{2}e^{2x}\qquad\text{ for every $x\geq 0$}.

(4.10)

It follows in particular that
(g^{\prime}e^{2x})^{\prime}\geq 0
, and
integrating this inequality between
0
and
x
leads to

g^{\prime}(x)\geq-e^{-2x}\lambda\qquad\text{ and }\qquad g(x)\geq-\frac{\lambda}{2}(1-e^{-2x}).

(4.11)

This lower bound on
g
gives an upper bound on
g^{2}
, and plugging this upper bound back into (
4.10
) gives

g^{\prime}(x)e^{2x}+\lambda\leq\int_{0}^{x}\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}\right)^{2}(e^{t}-e^{-t})^{2}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{4}\lambda^{2}(\sinh(2x)-2x).

Rearranging the above inequality, we have

g^{\prime}(x)\leq-\lambda e^{-2x}+\frac{1}{8}\lambda^{2}\left(1-e^{-4x}-4xe^{-2x}\right).

(4.12)

It follows that
g^{\prime}(x)
is negative for all
0\leq x\leq\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{8}{\lambda}
,
so that

x_{0}\geq\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{8}{\lambda},

(4.13)

yielding one side of the estimate on
x_{0}
claimed in the statement of the lemma.
Next, integrating (
4.12
) gives

g(x)\leq-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(1-e^{-2x}\right)+\frac{1}{8}\lambda^{2}\left(x-\frac{1-e^{-4x}}{4}+2xe^{-2x}-(1-e^{-2x})\right)\\
=-\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}-O\left(\lambda^{2}(1+x)\right)\right)\left(1-e^{-2x}\right),

(4.14)

where the implied constants in the big-
O
notation are uniform in
\lambda,x\geq 0
. Together with (
4.11
) this yields the bound on
g(x)
claimed in (
4.9
).
Finally, using the ODE (
4.10
), this upper bound on
g
can be used to lower bound
g^{\prime}
in exactly the same way that we previously obtained the upper bound (
4.12
) using the lower bound (
4.11
). This yields that there exists a positive constant
C_{1}
such that if
\lambda^{2}(1+x)\leq 1/C_{1}
then

g^{\prime}(x)\geq-\lambda e^{-2x}+\frac{1}{8}\lambda^{2}\left(1-C_{1}\lambda^{2}(1+x)\right)^{2}\left(1-e^{-4x}-4xe^{-2x}\right).

It follows that there exist positive constants
c
and
C_{2}
such that if
\lambda\leq c
then
g^{\prime}
must vanish at some point
x
smaller that
\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{8}{\lambda}+C_{2}\lambda\log\frac{1}{\lambda}
, and hence that

x_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{8}{\lambda}+C_{2}\lambda\log\frac{1}{\lambda}

for every
0\leq\lambda\leq c
as desired.
The estimate on the value of
g(x_{0})
follows from (
4.11
) and (
4.14
).
∎

We next study the second phase, where
g_{\lambda}
remains roughly constant.

Lemma 4.4

(Phase 2)
.

x_{1}-x_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}\log{\frac{O(1)}{\lambda}}
,

g_{\lambda}(x_{1})=-\left(1+O\left(\lambda|\log\lambda|\right)\right)\frac{\lambda}{2}
, and
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x_{1})=O(\lambda^{2})

as
\lambda\downarrow 0
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

By Lemma
4.2
,
g
is unimodal in the sense that
g^{\prime}>0
on
(x_{0},\infty)
. Since we also have by Lemma
3.12
that
|g(x)|\leq\lambda/2
for all
x\geq 0
, and since the maximum of
g^{\prime}(x)
is attained at the point
x_{1}
where
g^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})=0
and hence
2g^{\prime}(x_{1})=g(x_{1})^{2}\leq\lambda^{2}/4
, it follows that

g^{\prime}(x)\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{8},\quad\quad x\geq x_{0}.

(4.15)

Integrating shows that
g(x)-g(x_{0})\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{8}(x-x_{0})
for
x\geq x_{0}
. Together with the estimate on
g(x_{0})
from Lemma
4.3
, this yields the inequality

g(x)\leq-\frac{\lambda}{2}(1+O(\lambda|\!\log\lambda|)),\quad\quad x\in[x_{0},x_{0}+|\!\log\lambda|].

(4.16)

We can now integrate the differential inequality

(g^{\prime})^{\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2}\geq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}(1+O(\lambda|\log\lambda|))

between
x_{0}
and some
x\in[x_{0},x_{0}+|\log\lambda|]
(see Lemma
3.10
) to get that

g^{\prime}(x)\geq\frac{\lambda^{2}}{8}(1+O(\lambda|\log\lambda|))(1-e^{2(x_{0}-x)}),\quad x\in[x_{0},x_{0}+|\log\lambda|].

This estimate together with (
4.16
) implies the following differential inequality for
g^{\prime\prime}
on
[x_{0},x_{0}+|\log\lambda|]
:

(g^{\prime\prime})^{\prime}+2g^{\prime\prime}=2g^{\prime}g\leq-(1+O(\lambda|\log\lambda|))\frac{1}{8}\lambda^{3}(1-e^{2(x_{0}-x)}).

Integrating this inequality using Lemma
3.10
yields the following inequality for
x\in[x_{0},x_{0}+|\log\lambda|]
,

g^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq-(1+O(\lambda|\log\lambda|))\frac{\lambda^{3}}{16}+\left(g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})+(1+O(\lambda|\log\lambda|))\frac{\lambda^{3}}{16}(1+2(x-x_{0}))\right)e^{2(x_{0}-x)}.

By Lemma
4.3
,
g^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})=g(x_{0})^{2}=(1+O(\lambda))\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}
, so the above right hand side becomes negative as soon as
x\geq x_{0}+\frac{1}{2}|\log\lambda|+C
for a sufficiently large constant
C
. That is,

x_{1}-x_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}|\log\lambda|+O(1).

The estimates on
g(x_{1})
and
g^{\prime}(x_{1})
stated in Lemma
4.4
then follow from (
4.16
) and (
4.15
) respectively.
∎

Finally, we study the third phase, where
g_{\lambda}
has approximate power-law behaviour.

Lemma 4.5

(Phase 3)
.

Suppose that we take
x_{\lambda}\to\infty
as
\lambda\downarrow 0
in such a way that
\liminf(\log 1/\lambda)^{-1}x_{\lambda}>1
. Then

g_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})\sim-\frac{2\lambda}{\lambda x_{\lambda}+4}\qquad\text{as $\lambda\downarrow 0$.}

(4.17)

Moreover, the asymptotic estimate

g_{\lambda}(x)\sim-\frac{2}{x}\qquad\text{as $x\to\infty$}

(4.18)

holds for each fixed
\lambda\in(0,1)
.

A consequence of this lemma is that
g_{\lambda}
remains roughly constant from
x_{1}=O(\log 1/\lambda)
until the much later time when
x
is of order
1/\lambda
.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

We continue to write
g=g_{\lambda}
and define
\delta:=2g^{\prime}-g^{2}=-g^{\prime\prime}
. We first prove that

\sup_{x\geq x_{1}}|g^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq O(\lambda^{3})\qquad\text{as $\lambda\downarrow 0$.}

(4.19)

The lower bound
g^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq x_{1}
follows from Lemma
4.2
.
To prove the other bound, first observe that
\delta
is a nonnegative function on
[x_{1},\infty)
vanishing at
x_{1}
and converging to 0 at infinity (see Lemma
3.12
). Therefore, it reaches its maximum at some point
x_{2}>x_{1}
where
\delta^{\prime}=-2\delta-2gg^{\prime}
vanishes. This shows that
\max_{x\geq x_{1}}\delta(x)\leq\max_{x\geq x_{1}}|gg^{\prime}(x)|
.
Since
|g|
and
g^{\prime}
are both non increasing on
[x_{1},\infty)
we have that
\max_{x\geq x_{1}}|gg^{\prime}(x)|=|gg^{\prime}(x_{1})|
, and the claim follows from Lemma
4.4
.

We now use the estimate
\sup_{x\geq x_{1}}|g^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(x)|=O(\lambda^{3})
to prove (
4.17
). We begin by proving the claimed estimate under the additional assumption that
\limsup x_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda^{3}=0
; large values of
x_{\lambda}
satisfying
x_{\lambda}=\Omega(\lambda^{-3/2})
will be handled afterwards, simultaneously to the proof of (
4.18
). Since
g_{\lambda}
is increasing on
[x_{0},\infty)
and
x_{0}\sim\frac{1}{2}\log(1/\lambda)
as
\lambda\downarrow 0
, it suffices to prove the claim under the stronger assumption that
\liminf\lambda x_{\lambda}>0
.
Fix
\nu_{*}<1/2
; all implicit constants in the remainder of the proof may depend on
\nu_{*}
. Let
0<\nu<\nu_{*}
be a parameter and let
x(\nu):=\inf\{x>x_{1}:g(x)\geq-\nu\lambda\}
. On
[x_{1},x(\nu)]
, the absolute value
|g|
is larger than
\nu\lambda
, so (
4.19
) implies that

\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\leq g^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2}g^{2}+O(\lambda^{3})=(1+O(\nu^{-2}\lambda))\frac{1}{2}g^{2}\quad\text{on~}[x_{1},x(\nu)].

Integrating this relation between
x_{1}
and
x(\nu)
by considering the implied inequalities on the derivative of
1/g
yields

\frac{1}{2}(x(\nu)-x_{1})\leq\frac{1}{g(x_{1})}-\frac{1}{g(x_{2})}\leq(1+O(\nu^{-2}\lambda))\frac{1}{2}(x(\nu)-x_{1}).

(4.20)

Together with (
4.20
), this implies that

x(\nu)-x_{1}=(1+O((\nu^{-2}\lambda)\left[\frac{2}{g(x_{1})}-\frac{2}{g(x_{2})}\right]=(1+O(\nu^{-2}\lambda))\left(\frac{2}{\nu}-4\right)\frac{1}{\lambda}

where we safely absorbed the
1+O(\lambda)
prefactor on
g(x_{1})
into the
(1+O((\nu^{-2}\lambda))
prefactor in the second expression since
2/\nu\geq 2/\nu_{*}
is bounded away from
4
. On the other hand,
by Lemmas
4.3
and
4.4
,
x_{1}=o(1/\lambda)
, so that we can write simply

x(\nu)=(1+O(\nu^{-2}\lambda))\left(\frac{2}{\nu}-4\right)\frac{1}{\lambda}.

Inverting this expression yields the claim under the assumption that
\limsup x_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda^{3}=0
. (The assumption that
\liminf\lambda x_{\lambda}>0
, which we made at the beginning of this paragraph, lets us pick
\nu_{*}<1/2
and work only with
\nu<\nu_{*}
. The assumption that
\limsup x^{2}_{\lambda}\lambda^{3}=0
ensures that the
O(\nu^{-2}\lambda)=O(x^{2}(\nu)\lambda^{3})
term is negligible.)

We now prove (
4.18
) along with the case of (
4.17
) in which
\liminf x_{\lambda}\lambda=\infty
; together with our previous analysis of the case
\limsup x_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda^{3}
this establishes (
4.17
) in all relevant asymptotic regimes (indeed, there is a significant overlap in the regimes treated by the two arguments).
Since
g^{\prime\prime}=g^{2}-2g
is negative on
[x_{1},\infty)
,
we can integrate the relation
(-1/g)^{\prime}=g^{\prime}/g^{2}\geq 1/2
to obtain that

-\frac{1}{g(x)}+\frac{1}{g(x_{1})}\geq\frac{x-x_{1}}{2}

for every
x\geq x_{1}
, and hence that

g(x)\geq-\frac{2}{x-x_{1}-2/g(x_{1})}=-\frac{2}{x-O(\lambda^{-1})}

for every
x\geq x_{1}
, which yields the desired asymptotic lower bound when
\liminf x_{\lambda}\lambda=\infty
. We now prove the matching upper bound.
Fix
0<M<\infty
and let
x>x_{1}\vee\lambda^{-1}M
. Recall that
g=g_{\lambda}
is the unique solution to
g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2}
with
g(0)=0
and initial slope
g^{\prime}(0)=-\lambda
. Since
\lambda
is larger than
M/x
and smaller than
1
, Lemma
3.9
implies that
g
is bounded above by the solution with initial slope
-M/x
, and it follows from the
\limsup x_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda^{3}=0
case of the lemma treated above that

g(x)\leq g_{M/x}(x)\sim-\frac{2M}{(M+4)x}

as
x\to\infty
. The lower bound of the claimed estimate follows since
M
was arbitrary.
∎

4.3
Upper bounds on pioneers

In this section, we combine Theorem
3.1
and the results of Section
4.2
to establish a sharp upper bound on the tail of the number of pioneers. We then use this bound on the tail to bound from above the number of thick points, establishing the upper bound of Theorem
1.5
. We begin by proving the following proposition, which implies the upper bound of Theorem
1.3
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
x_{0}>0
. For each
\varepsilon>0
, there exist constants
C=C(\varepsilon)>0
and
x_{*}=x_{*}(x_{0},\varepsilon)>x_{0}
such that

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}\geq\frac{r^{2}\log(R/r)}{2}n\right)\leq\frac{C}{(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}\log(R/r)}e^{-(1-\varepsilon)n}

(4.21)

for every
1\leq r\leq e^{-x_{*}}R
and
n\geq 1
.

To see that this implies the upper bound of Theorem
1.3
, simply take
n=\lfloor a\log(R/r)\rfloor
and apply the hitting probability estimate (
4.2
)

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)\geq\frac{c(x_{0})}{(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}\log(R/r)}.

(4.22)

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

Let
x=\log(R/r)
so that
r=e^{-x}R
.
Let
x_{0}>0
, let
\mu=\mu(\varepsilon)>0
be large enough to ensure that
t:=\mu/(4+\mu)
is at least
1-\delta
, and let
x_{1}=x_{1}(\varepsilon,x_{0})>x_{0}
be such that if
x\geq x_{1}
then
\mu/x\leq 1/2
. (The constant
1/2
could be replaced by anything positive and strictly less than
1
.) Applying Theorem
3.1
with
\lambda=\mu/x
, we obtain that

\frac{1}{(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}g_{\mu/x}(x_{0})=1-\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\left(1-\frac{g_{\mu/x}(x)}{(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right)^{\!N_{r}}\,\right].

Using the asymptotics for
g_{\mu/x}(x)
proven in Lemma
4.5
and the bound
g_{\mu/x}(x_{0})\geq-\sup_{[0,\infty)}|g_{\mu/x}|\geq-\mu/(2x)
from Lemma
3.12
, we deduce that there exists a quantity
\delta(x,\mu)
satisfying
\lim_{x\to\infty}\delta(x,\mu)=0
such that

1-\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\left(1+\frac{(1+\delta(x,\mu))2\mu}{(\mu+4)x(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right)^{\!N_{r}}\,\right]\geq-\frac{\mu}{2x(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}

Recall that
t=\mu/(4+\mu)
, so that
\mu/2=2t/(1-t)
. Rewriting the above slightly, we obtain that there exists a quantity
\delta^{\prime}(x,\mu)
satisfying
\lim_{x\to\infty}\delta^{\prime}(x,\mu)=0
such that

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\exp\left((1+\delta^{\prime}(x,\mu))t\frac{2N_{r}}{x(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{N_{r}>0\right\}}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}>0\right)+\frac{t}{1-t}\frac{2}{x(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}.

By Lemma
4.1
, the probability that
N_{r}>0
is not larger than

(1+o(1))2/(x(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2})
as
x\to\infty
.
Using this and Markov’s inequality, we can now bound the left hand side of (
4.21
) by

\displaystyle e^{-(1+o(1))tn}\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\exp\Big((1+\delta^{\prime}(x,\mu))t\frac{2N_{r}}{x(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\left\{N_{r}>0\right\}}\right]\leq\frac{1+o(1)}{1-t}\frac{2}{x(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}e^{-t(1+o(1))n}.

Since we took
\mu
large enough to ensure that
t\geq 1-\delta
, this proves the proposition.
∎

We now explain how the upper bound of Theorem
1.5
follows from Proposition
4.6
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
– Upper bound.

We start by arguing that it is enough to show the upper bound with the killing on the outer sphere
\partial B(R)
(or more precisely, on
\partial B(AR)
for
A>0
large). Indeed, conditionally on surviving
R^{2}
generations, the probability that a particle reaches
\partial B(AR)
goes to zero as
R\to\infty
and
A\to\infty
. So we can
a priori
restrict ourselves to this event and add a killing on
\partial B(AR)
.

We now prove the upper bound under
\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}(\,\cdot\,|\zeta>R^{2})
, where we recall that
\zeta
denotes the extinction time of the branching process.
Let
a>0
. Recall the definition (
1.9
) of the set
\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)
of
a
-thick points. We compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{0},R}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\right\}}\right]\leq\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap B(0,R)}\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(N_{z,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right).

Let
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap B(0,R)
. By killing the particles on the sphere
\partial B(z,2R)
, which is strictly larger than the sphere
\partial B(R)
, we reduce the problem to a spherically symmetric situation once more. Proposition
4.6
then gives that

\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(N_{z,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right)\leq R^{-2-a+o(1)}.

Because
\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}(\zeta>R^{2})=R^{-2+o(1)}
(by Kolmogorov’s theorem about the extinction probability of a critical Bienaymé Galton–Watson tree; see e.g.
[
KNS66
, Corollary 1]
), the expectation of
\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)
conditional on
\{\zeta>R^{2}\}
is bounded by
R^{4-a+o(1)}
.
By Markov’s inequality, we deduce that for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\geq R^{4-a+\varepsilon}\,|\,\zeta>R^{2}\right)=0.

This gives the desired upper bound on the number of thick points. It also gives the upper bound on
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}N_{z,1}
since
\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)
is empty with high
\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}(\cdot|\zeta>R^{2})
-probability as
R\to\infty
for each
a>4
.
∎

5
Asymptotic expansion of solutions (proof of Theorem
1.7
)

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem, which establishes infinite-order asymptotic expansions for solutions to the ODEs satisfied by the functions
\mathsf{g}_{s}
and
\mathsf{h}_{s}
. The following theorem immediately implies
1.7
since

h(x)=e^{2x}\mathbb{P}_{e^{x},\infty}(N_{1}>0)

is a non-zero solution to the ODE
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
h
be any solution to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
that is not identically zero, and let
g
be any maximal solution to the initial value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2},\\
g(0)=0,~g^{\prime}(0)=-\lambda\end{array}\right.

with
\lambda\in(-1,0)
. Then
h(x)
is positive for all
x\geq 0
,
g(x)
is defined on
[0,\infty)
and negative for all
x\geq 0
, and there exist two sequences
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(P_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of polynomials such that for any
N\geq 1
,

g(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N})\quad\text{and}\quad h(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{P_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N}),\quad\quad\text{as~}x\to\infty.

Moreover, there exist constants
C_{\lambda},C_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
, with
C_{*}
depending on the choice of
h
, such that
Q_{1}=-2
,
Q_{2}(X)=2X+C_{\lambda}
,
P_{1}=2
,
P_{2}(X)=2X+C_{*}
and for all
n\geq 3
,
Q_{n}
and
P_{n}
are the unique polynomials satisfying the recurrence relations

(2n-4)Q_{n}-2Q_{n}^{\prime}=Q_{n-1}^{\prime\prime}-(2n-1)Q_{n-1}^{\prime}+n(n-1)Q_{n-1}-\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}Q_{k}Q_{n+1-k}

(5.1)

and

(2n-4)P_{n}-2P_{n}^{\prime}=-P_{n-1}^{\prime\prime}+(2n-1)P_{n-1}^{\prime}-(n-1)nP_{n-1}+\sum_{k=2}^{n-1}P_{k}P_{n+1-k}.

(5.2)

Remark 5.2
.

As already mentioned, the main difficulty in proving this theorem comes from the fact that the expansion
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{P_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}
diverges, i.e., is not summable for any finite
x
. Although we do not
prove
that this series diverges, we note that this feature is already present for the solutions of the linearised equation

h_{\text{lin}}^{\prime\prime}-2h_{\text{lin}}^{\prime}=\frac{2}{x}h_{\text{lin}},\quad h_{\text{lin}}(\infty)=0.

Indeed such solutions can be explicitly written in terms of the exponential integral
\text{Ei}(x)=-\int_{-x}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-t}}{t}\mathrm{d}t
:

h_{\text{lin}}(x)=c\left(1+2xe^{2x}\text{Ei}(-2x)\right),\quad c\in\mathbb{R},

from which we obtain that

h_{\text{lin}}(x)=c\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}(-1)^{n+1}\frac{n!}{(2x)^{n}}+O\left(N!(2x)^{-N}\right).

Although the series on the right hand side is also not a summable series, it is however a classical example of a Borel summable series. (Since we do not need this here, we do not explain the details of this notion. See
[
Har00
]
for background on this topic.)
This suggests that the series in Theorem
5.1
may also be summable in the Borel sense. We do not investigate this in this paper.

In particular, from Theorem
5.1
, we see that there is a constant
C_{\lambda}
such that

g_{\lambda}(x)=-\frac{2}{x}+\frac{2\log x+C_{\lambda}}{x^{2}}+o(x^{-2})\quad\quad\text{as}~x\to\infty.

(5.3)

The second goal of this section will be to study
the constant
C_{\lambda}
appearing in this asymptotic expansion.

The following lemma gives an alternative expression for
C_{\lambda}
and establishes asymptotic estimates on
C_{\lambda}
as
\lambda\downarrow 0
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For all
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
x_{0}>0
,

C_{\lambda}=-\int_{x_{0}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}+\frac{1}{x}\right)\mathrm{d}x-\frac{2}{g_{\lambda}(x_{0})}-x_{0}-\log x_{0}.

(5.4)

Moreover, the function
\lambda\in(0,1)\mapsto C_{\lambda}
is continuous, decreasing, and satisfies

C_{\lambda}\sim 4/\lambda
as
\lambda\downarrow 0
.

Figure 5.1
:
Left: Numerical plot of
x^{2}[g_{\lambda}(x)-(-\frac{2}{x}+\frac{2\log x}{x^{2}})]
with
x=1000
, which is a numerical approximation to
C_{\lambda}
, as a function of
\lambda
. Right: Numerical approximations to the difference of
g_{0.5}
and the three approximations
-2/x
(blue),
-2/x+2\log x/x^{2}
(red), and
-2/x+(2\log x+9.209)/x^{2}
(green), where the numerical value
C_{0.5}\approx 9.209
was extracted from the plot on the left.

The fact that the right hand side of (
5.4
) is independent of
x_{0}
follows from the fact that
g_{\lambda}
satisfies the ODE
g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}+2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}=g_{\lambda}^{2}
, so that
g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}/g_{\lambda}^{2}=1-2g^{\prime}_{\lambda}/g_{\lambda}^{2}=(x+2/g_{\lambda})^{\prime}
.

We prove Theorem
5.1
in Sections
5.1
and
5.2
and Lemma
5.3
in Section
5.3
.

5.1
Series expansion of
g

This section is dedicated to the proof of the part of Theorem
5.1
concerning the solutions of
g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2}
. The derivation of the asymptotic expansion of solutions to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
is closely analogous except that a different argument is required for the first-order estimate
h\sim 2/x
, and an appropriate replacement is needed for the unimodality properties proven for
g
in Lemma
4.2
; this is discussed in more detail in the next subsection.

We now outline the main steps of the proof. The first-order estimate
g_{\lambda}(x)\sim-2/x
was already proven in Lemma
4.5
.
In Lemma
5.4
, we warm up to the full asymptotic expansion by obtaining its second term, i.e., compute the asymptotic behaviour of
g
up to an error of order
o(x^{-2})
.
We will then show in Lemma
5.5
that the proof of this preliminary estimate can be generalised to obtain the existence of such an expansion for both
g_{\lambda}
and
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}
. The combinatorics of this step will be fairly involved and the induction relation (
5.1
) will be difficult to extract directly from the proof. Instead, we will show separately that if such an expansion holds for both
g_{\lambda}
and
g_{\lambda}^{\prime}
, then the polynomials
Q_{n}
have to satisfy (
5.1
). This final step will be straightforward since we will have already laid the groundwork to be able to differentiate term by term.

We now begin to carry out this plan. We begin by estimating
g_{\lambda}
and
g^{\prime}_{\lambda}
up to errors of order
o(x^{-2})
and
o(x^{-3})
respectively.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,1)
. There exists a constant
C_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{x}+\frac{2\log x+C_{\lambda}}{x^{2}}+o\left(\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)\qquad\text{and}

(5.5)

\displaystyle g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{x^{2}}-\frac{4\log x+2C_{\lambda}-2}{x^{3}}+o\left(\frac{1}{x^{3}}\right)

(5.6)

as
x\to\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

To lighten notation we write
g
instead of
g_{\lambda}
. We also define
\delta:=2g^{\prime}-g^{2}
. Although
\delta
is equal to
-g^{\prime\prime}
, we prefer to view it as the ‘error term’ describing the extend to which
g
fails to satisfy the separable ODE
2g^{\prime}=g^{2}
. The function
\delta
satisfies

\delta^{\prime}=-2\delta-2gg^{\prime}=-(2-|g|)\delta+|g|^{3}.

(5.7)

Define
x_{1}:=\inf\{x>0:\delta(x)=0\}
as in Section
4.2
. We showed in Lemma
4.2
that
x_{1}<\infty
and that
\delta
is nonnegative on
[x_{1},\infty)
.
We will iteratively prove a sequence of estimates on
g
and
\delta
of increasing strength.

Upper bound on
\delta
:
Using the estimate
g(x)\sim-2/x
in the differential equation (
5.7
), we obtain that

\delta^{\prime}(x)=-\left(2-\frac{2+o(1)}{x}\right)\delta(x)+\frac{8+o(1)}{x^{3}}\qquad\text{ as $x\to\infty$}.

(5.8)

and hence that if
x
is sufficiently large (so that the
(2+o(1))/x
term has absolute value at most
1
and the
(8+o(1))/x^{3}
term is at most
16/x^{3}
) then

\delta^{\prime}(x)+\delta(x)\leq\frac{16}{x^{3}}.

Let
x_{2}\geq x_{1}
be such that this estimate holds for all
x\geq x_{2}
.
After integration (Lemma
3.10
) this gives for all
x>x_{2}
,

\delta(x)\leq e^{-x}\int_{x_{2}}^{x}e^{t}\frac{16}{t^{3}}dt+e^{-x+x_{2}}\delta(x_{2})=O(x^{-3}).

We now plug this bound back into (
5.8
) to obtain that

\delta^{\prime}(x)+2\delta(x)\leq\frac{8+o(1)}{x^{3}}+O(x^{-4})=\frac{8+o(1)}{x^{3}}.

We integrate once more (using Lemma
3.10
) to obtain that

\delta(x)\leq e^{-2x}\int_{x_{2}}^{x}e^{2t}\left[\frac{8+o(1)}{x^{3}}\right]dt+e^{-2x+x_{2}}\delta(x_{2})=\frac{4+o(1)}{x^{3}},

(5.9)

where the final estimate follows by integration by parts.

Lower bound on
\delta
:

We now use the estimate
g(x)\sim-2/x
to prove a lower bound on
\delta
. Plugging the bound (
4.18
) into (
5.7
) and using that
|g|\delta\geq 0
yields that

\delta^{\prime}(x)+2\delta(x)\geq|g(x)|^{3}\geq\frac{8+o(1)}{x^{3}}

as
x\to\infty
,
and integrating this bound with Lemma
3.10
implies that

\delta(x)\geq e^{-2x}\int_{x_{2}}^{x}e^{2t}\left[\frac{4+o(1)}{x^{3}}\right]dt+e^{-2x+x_{2}}\delta(x_{2})=\frac{4+o(1)}{x^{3}}\qquad\text{as $x\to\infty$},

(5.10)

where again we use integration by parts to bound the relevant integrals. Together with (
5.9
) this yields that
\delta(x)\sim 4/x^{3}
as
x\to\infty
.

Improved lower bound on
g
:

We now use the estimate
\delta(x)\sim 4/x^{3}
to improve our lower bound on
g
. We have by (
5.9
) and (
4.18
) that

2g^{\prime}(x)-g(x)^{2}=\delta(x)\sim\frac{4+o(1)}{x^{3}}\sim\frac{1}{x}g(x)^{2}\qquad\text{as $x\to\infty$}

and hence that

-\left(\frac{1}{g(x)}+\frac{x}{2}\right)^{\prime}=\frac{g^{\prime}(x)}{g(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1+o(1)}{2x}\qquad\text{as $x\to\infty$.}

It follows in particular that if
x
is sufficiently large, then the left hand side term is at most
1/x

and integrating this bound yields that

|g(x)|\geq\frac{2}{x+2\log(x)+O(1)}\qquad\text{ as $x\to\infty$.}

(5.11)

Note that this is not yet a sharp estimate since the coefficient of
\log x
is
2
rather than
1
.

Completing the proof:
We are now ready to conclude the proof. Consider the telescoping sum

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{x}\frac{\delta(t)}{g(t)^{2}}\mathrm{d}t=\log x+\int_{1}^{x}\left(\delta(t)-\frac{4}{t^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{g(t)^{2}}\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{x}\frac{4}{t^{3}}\left(\frac{1}{g(t)^{2}}-\frac{t^{2}}{4}\right)\mathrm{d}t.

The estimates (
4.18
), (
5.9
), (
5.11
) and (
5.10
) on
|g|
and
\delta
show that the two integrals on the right hand side converge absolutely as
x\to\infty
. Therefore, there exists some
C_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\int_{1}^{x}\frac{\delta(t)}{g(t)^{2}}\mathrm{d}t=\log x+C_{0}+o(1)\quad\quad\text{as~}x\to\infty.

(5.12)

But by definition of
\delta
, the left hand side can also be expressed as

\int_{1}^{x}\frac{\delta(t)}{g(t)^{2}}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{1}^{x}\left[\frac{2g^{\prime}}{g(t)^{2}}-1\right]\mathrm{d}t=-\frac{2}{g(x)}+\frac{2}{g(1)}-(x-1).

Rearranging, we obtain that

g(x)=\frac{-2}{x+\log x+C_{0}-2/g(1)-1+o(1)}\qquad\text{ as $x\to\infty$,}

(5.13)

concluding the proof of (
5.5
). The estimate (
5.6
) follows and the relation
2g^{\prime}=g^{2}+\delta
since
\delta\sim 4/x^{3}
.
∎

We next extend this analysis to arbitrary order, without yet determining the relevant polynomials.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,1)
. There exist two sequences of polynomials
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(R_{n})_{n\geq 2}
such that for any
N\geq 1
,

g_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N}),\quad g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{n=2}^{N+1}\frac{R_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-1}),\quad\quad\text{as~}x\to\infty.

(5.14)

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

We will prove the statement by induction on
N
, the case
N=2
having already been treated in Lemma
5.4
. Let
N\geq 2
and suppose that there exist polynomials
(Q_{n})_{n=1,\ldots,N}
and
(R_{n})_{n=2,\ldots,N+1}
such that (
5.14
) holds. As in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we let
\delta:=2g^{\prime}-g^{2}
, which satisfies

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}+2\delta

\displaystyle=-2gg^{\prime}=-2\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}\right)\left(\sum_{n=2}^{N+1}\frac{R_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}\right)+o(x^{-N-2})=\sum_{n=3}^{N+2}\frac{Q_{n}^{(1)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-2}).

Here and in the rest of the proof, we write
(Q_{n}^{(1)})_{n\geq 1}
,
(Q_{n}^{(2)})_{n\geq 1}
, and so for certain polynomials arising in our computations. Although these polynomials can be written explicitly in terms of
(Q_{n})_{n=1\dots N}
and
(R_{n})_{n=2\dots N+1}
, we keep the dependence inexplicit for now and return to their precise computation later once we know we can differentiate term-by-term.
Using that
(e^{2x}\delta)^{\prime}=e^{2x}(\delta^{\prime}+2\delta)
, we can integrate this equality to obtain that

\displaystyle\delta(x)

\displaystyle=e^{-2x}\delta(1)+e^{-2x}\sum_{n=3}^{N+2}\int_{1}^{x}e^{2t}\frac{Q_{n}^{(1)}(\log t)}{t^{n}}\mathrm{d}t+e^{-2x}\int_{1}^{x}e^{2t}o(t^{-N-2})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=3}^{N+2}\frac{Q_{n}^{(2)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-2})

(5.15)

as
x\to\infty
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(x)}{g(x)^{2}}=(1+o(x^{-N+1}))\left(\sum_{n=3}^{N+2}\frac{Q_{n}^{(2)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}\right)\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}\right)^{-2}.

Since
Q_{1}=-2
is a constant function, we can expand the negative binomial to rewrite this as

\frac{\delta(x)}{g(x)^{2}}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}^{(3)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N})=\frac{1}{x}+\sum_{n=2}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}^{(3)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N}),

(5.16)

where we used the first-order asymptotics for
\delta
and
g
computed in Lemma
5.4
to compute the first term of the asymptotic series.
Note that here it is crucial that the polynomial
Q_{1}
is constant (equal to
-2
), otherwise the above negative-binomial expansion would involve rational functions of
\log x
instead of polynomials.

The estimate (
5.16
) implies that

\int_{x}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta(t)}{g(t)^{2}}-\frac{1}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{n=2}^{N}\int_{x}^{\infty}\frac{Q_{n}^{(3)}(\log t)}{t^{n}}\mathrm{d}t+o(x^{-N+1})=\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\frac{Q_{n}^{(4)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N+1})

as
x\to\infty
, where the expansion of the integrals appearing here can be established using iterated integration by parts. Meanwhile, the definition of
\delta
and the preliminary estimate we obtained in Lemma
5.4
yield that

\lim_{y\to\infty}\int_{x}^{y}\left(\frac{\delta(t)}{g(t)^{2}}-\frac{1}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t=\lim_{y\to\infty}\left(\frac{2}{g(x)}-\frac{2}{g(y)}-(y-x)-\log\frac{y}{x}\right)=\frac{C_{\lambda}}{2}+\frac{2}{g(x)}+x+\log x,

so that

-\frac{2}{g(x)}=x+\log x+\frac{C_{\lambda}}{2}-\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\frac{Q_{n}^{(4)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N+1})

as
x\to\infty
.
By using once more the power series of
(1+u)^{-1}
, this gives exactly the estimate we wanted for
g
:

g(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{N+1}\frac{Q_{n}^{(5)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-1}).

The estimate for
g^{\prime}
is then a consequence of this estimate together with (
5.15
):

2g^{\prime}(x)=\delta(x)+g(x)^{2}=\sum_{n=1}^{N+2}\frac{Q_{n}^{(6)}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-2}).

This concludes our inductive proof.
∎

We conclude this section by proving the part of Theorem
5.1
concerning
g
:

Proof of Theorem
5.1
for the function
g
.

By using the asymptotic expansions of
g
and
g^{\prime}
from Lemma
5.5
and by using the fact that
g^{\prime\prime}=g^{2}-2g^{\prime}
, we obtain for free an asymptotic expansion for
g^{\prime\prime}
of a similar form. Since
g
and
g^{\prime}
both converge to zero as
x\to\infty
, we can write
g^{\prime}(x)=-\int_{x}^{\infty}g^{\prime\prime}(t)\mathrm{d}t
and
g(x)=-\int_{x}^{\infty}g^{\prime}(t)\mathrm{d}t
, which allows us to reobtain the asymptotic expansions of
g
and
g^{\prime}
by integrating the asymptotic expansion of
g^{\prime\prime}
term by term.
Wrapping up, we have shown that there exists a sequence of polynomials
(Q_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that for any
N\geq 1
,

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{Q_{n}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N})

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=2}^{N+1}\frac{Q^{\prime}_{n-1}(\log x)-(n-1)Q_{n-1}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-1})

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=3}^{N+2}\frac{Q_{n-2}^{\prime\prime}(\log x)-(2n-3)Q_{n-2}^{\prime}(\log x)+(n-1)(n-2)Q_{n-2}(\log x)}{x^{n}}+o(x^{-N-2}).

Because
g
satisfies
g^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}=g^{2}
, we must have
2Q_{1}^{\prime}-2Q_{1}=Q_{1}^{2}
and that

\displaystyle Q_{n-2}^{\prime\prime}-(2n-3)Q_{n-2}^{\prime}+(n-1)(n-2)Q_{n-2}+2Q_{n-1}^{\prime}-2(n-1)Q_{n-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}Q_{k}Q_{n-k}

for every
n\geq 3
.
It follows from Lemma
5.4
that
Q_{1}=-2
and
Q_{2}(X)=2X+C_{\lambda}
,
and rearranging the above display leads to the recurrence (
5.1
). One can also easily check that this recurrence relation uniquely determines
Q_{n}
in terms of
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{n-1}
for every
n\geq 2
, since the matrix that must be inverted to solve for the coefficients of
Q_{n}
is upper-triangular with diagonal terms all equal to
2n-4
.
(For
n=2
the term
(2n-4)Q_{n}
vanishes and the recurrence relation does not put any constraints on the constant term of
Q_{2}
.) This concludes the proof.
∎

5.2
Series expansion of
h

In this section we explain how to modify the analysis of
g
carried out in the previous section to derive the series expansion for
h
. We will focus on the first-order asymptotics
h(x)\sim 2/x
together with the appropriate replacement for the unimodality properties of
g
derived in Lemma
4.2
(namely, the fact that
h
is totally monotone), with the rest of the proof being very similar.

We begin with the following proposition, which establishes non-negativity of all solutions to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on all of
[0,\infty)
together with the fact that these solutions are always totally monotone. The latter property replaces the unimodality properties of
g
established in Lemma
4.2
. The fact that these properties hold for
all
solutions defined on
[0,\infty)
indicates an important difference between the two ODEs (or equivalently between the forward and backward evolution of either ODE).

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
h
be any solution to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
. If
h
is not identically zero then the following hold:

1.

h(x)>0
for all
x\geq 0
.

2.

h(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
.

3.

h
is totally monotone in the sense that
(-1)^{n}h^{(n)}(x)>0
for every
n\geq 1
.

4.

h^{(n)}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
for every
n\geq 1
.

We note that this proposition together with the uniqueness of non-negative solutions to boundary value problems for the ODE
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
(which can be proven as in Lemma
2.6
) has the following simple corollary.

Corollary 5.7
.

There exists a totally monotone function
h_{\infty}:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
with
h_{\infty}(x)\to\infty
as
x\to 0
and
h_{\infty}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
such that if
h
is any solution to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
then there exists
c>0
such that
h(x)=h_{\infty}(x+c)
for every
x\geq 0
.

Remark 5.8
.

The quantity
h_{\infty}(x)
can be written in terms of the measure given to the set
\{
the unit ball is hit
\}
by the four-dimensional superBrownian excursion measure started at
e^{x}
.

We break the proof of Proposition
5.6

into a few steps, beginning with the following lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
h
be any solution to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
. If
h
is not identically zero then
h^{\prime}(x)<0
for all
x\geq 0
.

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

Suppose not, so that there exists
x_{0}\geq 0
with
h^{\prime}(x_{0})\geq 0
. Since
h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})=2h^{\prime}(x_{0})+h(x_{0})^{2}\geq 0
and
h^{\prime}
cannot vanish on any non-trivial interval (by uniqueness of forward and backward solutions to the IVP associated to the ODE), there must exist
x_{1}>x_{0}
with
h^{\prime}(x_{1})>0
. Using that
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}\geq 0
, it follows by Lemma
3.10
that
h^{\prime}(x)\geq e^{2(x-x_{1})}
for every
x\geq x_{1}
. Integrating this inequality, it follows that there exists
x_{2}\geq x_{1}
such that
h^{\prime}(x)
and
h(x)
are both bounded below by
1
for all
x\geq x_{2}
, and hence that
h^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq x_{2}
.
Now, let
y_{0}=x_{2}
and for each
n\geq 1
let
y_{n}\geq y_{n-1}
be minimal such that
h^{\prime}(y_{n})=2^{n}
. For each
x\geq y_{n}
we have that

h(x)=h(y_{n})+\int_{y_{n}}^{x}h^{\prime}(t)\mathrm{d}t\geq 2^{n}(x-y_{n})

and hence by Lemma
3.10
that

h^{\prime}(x)\geq e^{2(x-y_{n})}h(y_{n})+2^{2n}\int_{y_{n}}^{x}(t-y_{n})^{2}\mathrm{d}t\geq\frac{1}{3}2^{2n}(x-y_{n})^{3}

for every
x\geq y_{n}
. This implies that

y_{n+1}-y_{n}\leq(3\cdot 2^{-n})^{1/3}

for every
n\geq 0
, and since
\sum(3\cdot 2^{-n})^{1/3}<\infty
this shows that
h^{\prime}
blows up in finite time and contradicts the assumption that
h
is defined on all of
[0,\infty)
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
h
be any solution to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
. If
h
is not identically zero then
h(x)>0
for all
x\geq 0
and
h(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
5.10
.

Since
h^{\prime}(x)<0
for all
x\geq 0
,
h(x)
converges to some
c\in[-\infty,\infty)
as
x\to\infty
, and it suffices to prove that
c=0
. We will rule out the cases
c>0
and
c<0
separately.

First suppose for contradiction that
c
is positive, in which case
h(x)
is positive for all
x\geq 0
. We first claim that
h^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq 0
for every
x\geq 0
. Suppose that this is not the case, so that there exists
x_{0}\geq 0
with
h^{\prime\prime}(x)<0
. The second derivative
h^{\prime\prime}
satisfies the differential inequality
h^{\prime\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime\prime}=2hh^{\prime}\leq 0
, so that (by Lemma
3.10
)
h^{\prime\prime}(x)\leq e^{2(x-x_{0})}h^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})
for every
x\geq x_{0}
. Integrating this inequality twice implies that
h(x)
is eventually negative, contradicting the assumption that
c\geq 0
. This completes the proof that the second derivative is non-negative. Now, since
h(x)\to c
and
h^{\prime}(x)
is monotone, we must have that
h^{\prime}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
. But the ODE then implies that
h^{\prime\prime}(x)\to c^{2}>0
as
x\to\infty
, a contradiction. This rules out the case that
c
is positive.

Now suppose that
c
is negative, and let
x_{0}\geq 0
be such that
h(x)\leq 0
for all
x\geq x_{0}
. In this case, the second derivative satisfies the differential inequality
h^{\prime\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime\prime}=2hh^{\prime}\geq 0
for every
x\geq x_{0}
, and it follows by a similar argument to above that
h^{\prime\prime}(x)<0
for all
x>x_{0}
. As such,
h^{\prime}(x)\leq h^{\prime}(x_{0})<0
for all
x\geq x_{0}
, so that
c
cannot be finite and negative.

We now argue as in the proof of Lemma
5.9
that if
c=-\infty
then in fact
h^{\prime}
blows up to negative infinity in finite time. Let
x_{1}\geq x_{0}
be such that
h(x)\leq-1
for all
x\geq x_{1}
and, for each
n\geq 0
let
y_{n}\geq x_{0}
be minimal such that
h^{\prime}(y_{n})\leq-2^{n}
. Integrating the differential inequality
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}\geq 1
on
[x_{1},\infty)
yields that
y_{n}<\infty
for every
n\geq 0
. Since
h^{\prime}
is decreasing on
[x_{1},\infty)
, it follws as in the proof of Lemma
5.9
that

h(x)\leq-2^{n}(x-y_{n})\text{ and that }h^{\prime}(x)\leq-2^{2n}\int_{y_{n}}^{x}(t-y_{n})^{3}

for every
n\geq 0
and
x\geq y_{n}
, so that
y_{n+1}\leq(3\cdot 2^{-n})^{1/3}
for every
n\geq 0
. It follows as before that
h^{\prime}
blows up to negative infinity in finite time, contradicting the assumption that
h
was defined on
[0,\infty)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.6
.

We have already shown that
h(x)>0
for all
x\geq 0
,
h^{\prime}(x)<0
for all
x\geq 0
, and that
h(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
.
We now prove by induction on
n
that
(-1)^{n}h^{(n)}(x)>0
for all
x\geq 0
and that
h^{(n)}(x)\to 0
. Suppose that
n\geq 2
and that
(-1)^{n}h^{(k)}(x)>0
and
h^{(k)}(x)\to 0
for all
0\leq k<n
and
x\geq 0
. The
n
th derivative
h^{(n)}
satisfies

h^{(n+1)}-2h^{(n)}=(h^{2})^{(n-1)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{k}h^{(k)}h^{(n-1-k)}.

(5.17)

By the induction hypothesis, the right hand side is positive if
n
is odd and negative if
n
is even. Thus, it follows by Lemma
3.10
that if
(-1)^{n}h^{(n)}(x_{0})\leq 0
for some
x_{0}\geq 0
then
(-1)^{n}h^{(n)}(x)\leq 0
for all
x\geq x_{0}
. This is inconsistent with the induction hypothesis that
(-1)^{n-1}h^{(n-1)}(x)
converges to
0
from above as
x\to\infty
, so that we must have
(-1)^{n}h^{(n)}(x)>0
for all
x\geq 0
as claimed.

It remains to prove that
h^{(n)}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
. Since
h^{(n-1)}(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
, we must have that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}|h^{(n)}(x)|=0
.
By (
5.17
) and the induction hypothesis, there exists
x_{0}<\infty
such that

|h^{(n+1)}(x)-2h^{(n)}(x)|\leq\varepsilon

for all
x\geq x_{0}
. Applying Lemma
3.10
, we deduce that

|h^{(n)}(x)-e^{2(x-x_{1})}h^{(n)}(x_{1})|\leq\varepsilon\int_{x_{1}}^{x}e^{2x-2t}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e^{2(x-x_{1})}

for every
x\geq x_{1}\geq x_{0}
. Thus, if
x_{1}\geq x_{0}
is such that
|h^{(n)}(x_{1})|\geq\varepsilon
then
|h^{(n)}(x)|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}e^{2(x-x_{1})}
for every
x\geq x_{1}
, contradicting the fact that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}|h^{(n)}(x)|=0
. This completes the proof of the induction step and hence of the proposition.
∎

We now compute the first-order asymptotics of
h
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
h
be any solution to
h^{\prime\prime}-2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
defined on
[0,\infty)
. If
h
is not identically zero then
h(x)\sim 2/x
as
x\to\infty
.

Proof.

Define
\delta=h^{\prime\prime}=2h^{\prime}+h^{2}
. Since
h
is totally monotone and
\delta=h^{\prime\prime}
, we have that
\delta(x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq 0
and hence that
h
satisfies the differential inequality
2h^{\prime}\geq-h^{2}
. Integrating the resulting inequality on the derivative of
1/h
yields that

\frac{1}{h(x)}-\frac{1}{h(0)}=\int_{0}^{x}-\frac{h^{\prime}(t)}{h^{2}(t)}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{x}{2}

and hence that

h(x)\geq\frac{2+o(1)}{x}

as
x\to\infty
.

We now prove the matching upper bound. We first claim that
\delta(x)=|h^{\prime\prime}(x)|=o(|h^{\prime}(x)|)
as
x\to\infty
.
Since
h
is totally monotone,
|h^{\prime\prime}(x)/h^{\prime}(x)|
is decreasing and hence converges from above to some limit
c\geq 0
. If
c>0
then we have that
h^{\prime\prime}(x)\geq-ch^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\geq 0
and hence by Grönwall (Lemma
3.10
) that
|h^{\prime}(x)|\leq e^{-cx}|h^{\prime}(0)|
for every
x\geq 0
. Since
h(x)\to 0
as
x\to\infty
, this inequality is inconsistent with the sub-exponential lower bound
h(x)\geq(2+o(1))/x
, so that we must have
c=0
as claimed. Using that
h^{\prime\prime}(x)=o(|h^{\prime}(x)|)
as
x\to\infty
, we can approximately simplify the ODE
h^{\prime\prime}+2h^{\prime}=h^{2}
by writing

h^{\prime}(x)\sim-\frac{1}{2}h^{2}.

The claim follows by integrating the resulting asymptotic estimate
(1/h)^{\prime}\sim 1/2
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

It should be possible to adapt this proof to give an alternative derivation of the first-order asymptotics of
g_{\lambda}
also.

Given Propositon
5.6
and Lemma
5.11
, the rest of the proof of the part of Theorem
5.1
concerning
h
is very similar to the part concerning
g
, and we omit the details.

5.3
Proof of Lemma
5.3

In this section we prove Lemma
5.3
, providing an integral expression for the constant
C_{\lambda}
which allows us to deduce continuity of
\lambda
and the asymptotic expression
C_{\lambda}\sim 4/\lambda
as
\lambda\downarrow 0
.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

As usual, we will denote
\delta_{\lambda}=2g_{\lambda}^{\prime}-g_{\lambda}^{2}=-g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}
.
By (
5.12
) and (
5.13
),
C_{\lambda}
can be written as

C_{\lambda}=\int_{1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}\right)\mathrm{d}x-\frac{2}{g_{\lambda}(1)}-1.

(5.18)

The first integration bound was chosen in an arbitrary way, meaning that for any
x_{0}>0
,
C_{\lambda}
can be written as

C_{\lambda}=\int_{x_{0}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}\right)\mathrm{d}x-\frac{2}{g_{\lambda}(x_{0})}-x_{0}-\log x_{0}.

(5.19)

This corresponds to the formula stated in (
5.4
). We emphasise that the term
\log x_{0}
was not apparent when
x_{0}
was chosen to be equal to 1 since
\log 1=0
. (Note that the equation
g_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}+2g^{\prime}_{\lambda}=g_{\lambda}^{2}
easily implies that the expression on the right hand side of the above equation does not depend on
x_{0}
.)

We now prove continuity of
C_{\lambda}
. We use (
5.18
).
The maps
\lambda\mapsto g_{\lambda}(1)
and
