\lambda\mapsto\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}
,
x>1
, are continuous since solutions to initial value problems depend continuously on their initial conditions.
By (
5.4
) and the dominated convergence theorem, to conclude that
C_{\lambda}
is continuous it is enough to check that for each
\lambda\in(0,1)
there is a neighbourhood of
\lambda
on which
\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}
is dominated by some integrable function.
Let
0<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<1
. By Theorem
3.1
,
g_{\lambda}(x)
is monotone in
\lambda
, so for all
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
,

|g_{\lambda_{1}}(x)|\leq|g_{\lambda}(x)|\leq|g_{\lambda_{2}}(x)|,

meaning that we have uniform control on
g_{\lambda}(x)
in the sense that

\sup_{\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}|g_{\lambda}(x)|=|g_{\lambda_{2}}(x)|=\frac{2}{x}+O\left(\frac{\log x}{x^{2}}\right)

and

\inf_{\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}|g_{\lambda}(x)|=|g_{\lambda_{1}}(x)|=\frac{2}{x}+O\left(\frac{\log x}{x^{2}}\right).

Here and below we use the notation that
f(x)=O(g(x))
to mean that there exists there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|f(x)|\leq C|g(x)|
. In particular the reader should keep in mind that the left hand side might well be negative.

Since we obtained the bounds (
5.9
) and (
5.10
) on
\delta_{\lambda}
using only first-order estimates on
|g_{\lambda}|
, we deduce that the same bounds on
\delta
hold uniformly in
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
in the sense that

\inf_{\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}\delta_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{4}{x^{3}}+O\left(\frac{\log x}{x^{4}}\right)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sup_{\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]}\delta_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{4}{x^{3}}+O\left(\frac{\log x}{x^{4}}\right)

as
x\to\infty
. It follows that

\left|\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}\right|=O\left(\frac{\log x}{x^{2}}\right)

uniformly in
\lambda\in[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]
. This provides the required domination by an integrable function and concludes the proof of continuity of
C_{\lambda}
. The fact that
C_{\lambda}
is decreasing on
(0,1)
follows immediately from the fact that
g_{\lambda}(x)
is a decreasing function of
\lambda\in(0,1)
as proven in Lemma
3.9
.

We now show that
C_{\lambda}\sim 4/\lambda
as
\lambda\downarrow 0
. Roughly speaking, we will check that the asymptotic expansion (
5.3
) is a good approximation as soon as
x\geq 1/\lambda
and conclude using Lemma
4.5
. The following line of argument is close to the proof of Lemma
5.4
.
Let
\lambda\in(0,1/2]
. All the big-O written in the rest of the proof are uniform with respect to
\lambda
. Let
x_{\lambda}=1/\lambda
and
x\geq x_{\lambda}
. As noticed above, the upper bounds on
|g_{\lambda}|
and
|\delta_{\lambda}|
are uniform in
\lambda(0,1/2]
:

|g_{\lambda}(x)|\leq|g_{1/2}(x)|\leq\frac{2}{x}+O(1)\frac{\log x}{x}

(5.20)

and

\delta(x)\leq\frac{4}{x^{3}}+O(1)\frac{\log x}{x^{4}}.

(5.21)

Obtaining uniform lower bounds require more care. (Indeed, we recall from Lemma
3.12
that
g_{\lambda}\to 0
pointwise as
\lambda\to 0
.) First, notice that since
1/x\leq\lambda
,

|g_{\lambda}(x)|\geq|g_{\lambda=1/x}(x)|\sim\frac{2}{5x}\quad\quad\text{as}~x\to\infty

by Lemma
4.5
. Hence, there exists
\lambda_{0}\in(0,1/2]
small enough, such that for all
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{0}]
and
x\geq x_{\lambda}
,
|g_{\lambda}(x)|\geq 1/(5x)
. Injecting this estimate in (
5.21
) shows that

-\left(\frac{1}{g_{\lambda}(x)}+\frac{x}{2}\right)^{\prime}=\frac{g_{\lambda}^{\prime}(x)}{g(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{2g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}\leq\frac{O(1)}{x},\quad\quad x\geq x_{\lambda},

where we stress again that all big-
O
estimates are uniform in
\lambda\in(0,1/2]
and
x\geq x_{\lambda}
.
Integrating between
x_{\lambda}
and some
x\geq x_{\lambda}
shows that

|g_{\lambda}(x)|\geq\frac{2}{x+O(1)\log(x/x_{\lambda})+2/|g_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})|-x_{\lambda}}.

By Lemma
4.5
, the difference
2/|g_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})|-x_{\lambda}
is asymptotic to
4/\lambda
as
\lambda\to 0
. Using in particular that it is bounded by
O(1/\lambda)
, we can inject this improved lower bound on
|g_{\lambda}|
back in (
5.21
) to obtain that

-\left(\frac{1}{g_{\lambda}(x)}+\frac{x}{2}\right)^{\prime}=\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{2g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2x}+\frac{O(1)}{\lambda x^{2}}+O(1)\frac{\log x}{x^{2}},\quad\quad x\geq x_{\lambda}.

Integrating this inequality finally shows that

|g_{\lambda}(x)|\geq\frac{2}{x+\log(x/x_{\lambda})+2/|g_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})|-x_{\lambda}+O(1)/(\lambda x)+O(1)\log x/x}.

In particular,

C_{\lambda}=\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2}{|g_{\lambda}(x)|}-x-\log x\leq\frac{2}{|g_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})|}-x_{\lambda}-\log x_{\lambda}.

We can now use this lower bound on
|g_{\lambda}|
to get a lower bound on
\delta_{\lambda}
using the differential inequality
\delta_{\lambda}^{\prime}+2\delta_{\lambda}=|g_{\lambda}|\delta+|g_{\lambda}|^{3}\geq|g_{\lambda}|^{3}
, which follows from (
5.7
). We get that

\delta_{\lambda}(x)\geq\frac{4}{x^{3}}+O(1)\frac{1}{\lambda x^{4}}+O(1)\frac{\log x}{x^{4}},\quad\quad x\geq 2x_{\lambda}.

See the paragraph around
(
5.10
) for details. This lower bound on
\delta_{\lambda}
together with the upper bound (
5.20
) on
|g_{\lambda}|
shows that

\int_{2x_{\lambda}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}\right)\mathrm{d}x\geq O(1)\int_{2x_{\lambda}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda x^{2}}+\frac{\log x}{x^{2}}\right)\mathrm{d}x\geq O(1)\frac{1}{\lambda x_{\lambda}}+O(1)\frac{\log x_{\lambda}}{x_{\lambda}}\geq O(1).

Applying the formula (
5.4
) for
C_{\lambda}
to
x_{0}=2x_{\lambda}
, we get that

\displaystyle C_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|g_{\lambda}(2x_{\lambda})|}-2x_{\lambda}-\log 2x_{\lambda}+\int_{2x_{\lambda}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta_{\lambda}(x)}{g_{\lambda}(x)^{2}}-\frac{1}{x}\right)\mathrm{d}x\geq\frac{2}{|g_{\lambda}(2x_{\lambda})|}-2x_{\lambda}-\log 2x_{\lambda}+O(1).

Wrapping up, we have proved that

\frac{2}{|g_{\lambda}(2x_{\lambda})|}-2x_{\lambda}-\log 2x_{\lambda}+O(1)\leq C_{\lambda}\leq\frac{2}{|g_{\lambda}(x_{\lambda})|}-x_{\lambda}-\log x_{\lambda}.

By Lemma
4.5
, both sides of the above inequalities are asymptotically equivalent to
4/\lambda
as
\lambda\to 0
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6
Tail estimates with killing II: Lower bounds (proof of Theorem
1.3
)

The aim of this section is to prove the lower bound of Theorems
1.3
, completing the proof of that theorem. The proof relies on the following precise asymptotic of the Laplace transform of the number of pioneers, which we prove in this section using Theorem
1.7
as an input.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
x_{0}>0
. For each
\delta>0
, there exist constants
T_{0},R_{0}>0
and
x_{*}>x_{0}
depending only on
x_{0}
and
\delta
such that

e^{-\delta}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}x}\frac{x}{T}\leq\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{T}{x}\right)\frac{2N_{e^{-x}R}}{(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right\}-1\right]\leq e^{\delta}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}x}\frac{x}{T}

(6.1)

for all
T\in[T_{0},2T_{0}]
,
R\geq R_{0}
, and
x\in[x_{*},\log R]
.

Remark 6.2
.

As we will see in Lemma
4.1
below, the term
\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}x}
corresponds to the asymptotic behaviour of the hitting probability of
\partial B(0,e^{-x}R)
starting from a single particle on
\partial B(0,e^{-x_{0}}R)
. Therefore, Theorem
6.1
says in a very strong and precise way that the law of the number of pioneers
N_{e^{-x}R}
conditioned to be positive is close to that of an exponential random variable with mean
\frac{1}{2}(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}
.

The logarithmic shift in the mean (which corresponds to shifting by
\log\log R
in the original scale) is important in the sense that the theorem would not be true without it. This logarithmic shift will eventually come from the second order term in the expansion in Theorem
5.1
. To see why it matters, one simply needs to expand

\left(1-\frac{T}{x}\right)\frac{1}{x+\log x}=\frac{1}{x}-\frac{\log x}{x^{2}}-\frac{T}{x^{2}}+\cdots.

The dependence in
T
, which is the key feature of this theorem, appears only from the third term onward; if we replaced
\log x
by any other quantity differing by a divergent term, this effect would overwhelm that of changing
T
.

Before proving Theorem
6.1
, we will first explain how we will use it to deduce Theorem
1.3
. The idea is of course to convert information on the Laplace transform into information on the tail. This is usually achieved by the means of the classical Tauberian theorem of Hardy–Littlewood. However, the assumptions of that theorem are too restrictive to apply given only the information we obtain in Theorem
6.1
. Roughly speaking, in order to use such a theorem we would require asymptotics of the Laplace transform for values of
1-\tfrac{T}{x}
arbitrarily close to
1
.
In our case, even if we take
x
as large as possible (namely, take
x=\log R
so that we consider the pioneers on the ball of radius
1
) our estimates on the Laplace transform hold only on an interval of the type
[1-t_{1}/\log R,1-t_{2}/\log R]
. The Hardy–Littlewood theorem also requires an assumption of regular or slow variation, something which we do not have at our disposal.
To proceed, we therefore require a finitary improvement of the classical Tauberian theorem.
Fortunately, we manage to prove such a finitary improvement using fairly soft arguments in Section

6.2
; we anticipate that this will be useful in other problems too. This is an improvement over the classical theorem in the sense that the assumptions are less restrictive, but of course this is reflected in the conclusion too, which is not as strong.

We prove Theorem
6.1
in Section
6.1
, formulate and prove our finitary Tauberian theorem in Section
6.2
, and then deduce Theorem
1.2
in Section
6.3
.

6.1
Estimates on the Laplace transform

In this section we prove Theorem
6.1
. The proof will rely on the results of Sections
4
and
5
.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

To ease notations, we will prove the statement only for
x=L
so that
e^{-x}R=1
. The same line of argument proves the result in general: Simply replace the number of pioneers
N_{1}
on the unit sphere by the renormalised number of pioneers
N_{e^{-x}R}/(e^{-x}R)^{2}
on the sphere
\partial B(e^{-x}R)
and each occurrence of
L
by
x
. Throughout the proof, we will write
e^{\pm\delta}
for a quantity that is bounded between
e^{-\delta}
and
e^{\delta}
, the precise value of which may vary from line to line.

We first introduce some parameters.
Let
x_{0}>0
be fixed, and let
\delta>0
be small.
Let us first pick
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}(\delta)
small enough so that, defining
A:=1/\mu_{0}
,

\frac{1}{\delta}e^{-\delta A/2}\leq\delta\quad\text{and}\quad A\geq 8e^{10\delta}.

(6.2)

By Lemmas
4.5
and
5.3
, for all
\mu>0
, we have that

\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\xi g_{\mu/\xi}(\xi)=\frac{-2}{1+4/\mu}\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\xi\to\infty}\frac{1}{\xi}C_{\mu/\xi}=\frac{4}{\mu}

uniformly with respect to
\mu
on compact subsets of
(0,\infty)
. Thus, by choosing
\mu_{0}
smaller if necessary, we can find
\xi=\xi(\delta,\mu_{0})\geq\max\{x_{0},3\mu_{0}\}
large such that if
\mu\in[\mu_{0},3\mu_{0}]
then

\frac{e^{-\delta}}{C_{\lambda=\mu/\xi}}\leq-\frac{1}{2}g_{\lambda=\mu/\xi}(\xi)\leq\frac{e^{\delta}}{C_{\lambda=\mu/\xi}}\quad\text{and}\quad 4e^{-\delta}\frac{\xi}{\mu}\leq C_{\lambda=\mu/\xi}\leq 4e^{\delta}\frac{\xi}{\mu}.

(6.3)

Moreover, by Proposition
4.6
applied with
\varepsilon=1/2
, we may assume that
\xi
is large enough (depending only on
x_{0}
and
\delta
) so that for all
n\geq 1
,

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-\xi}R}\geq\frac{\xi(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}}{2}n\right)\leq\frac{C(x_{0})}{\xi(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}e^{-n/2};\quad\text{ and }\quad e^{-2\xi}\leq 1-e^{-\delta}

(6.4)

for some constant
C(x_{0})
depending only
x_{0}
.

Now that our parameters are defined, we can start more concretely the proof.
Since
\xi\geq 3\mu_{0}
, it follows from Theorem
3.1
that

1-\mathbb{E}_{e^{-\xi}R,R}\left[\left(1-g_{\lambda}(L)\right)^{N}\right]=\frac{1}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}}g_{\lambda}(\xi)

for all
\lambda\in[\mu_{0}/\xi,3\mu_{0}/\xi]
. Applying Theorem
5.1
(more precisely (
5.3
)), it follows that

g_{\lambda}(L)=\frac{-2}{L+\log L+C_{\lambda}+o(1)}

as
L\to\infty
,
where, here and in what follows, all uses of asymptotic notation are taken to be uniform in
\lambda\in[\mu_{0}/\xi,3\mu_{0}/\xi]
(the fact that the estimate is uniform in this range follows by continuity of
C_{\lambda}
). Writing

N\log\left(1+\frac{2}{L+\log L+C_{\lambda}+o(1)}\right)=\left(1-\frac{C_{\lambda}+o(1)}{L}\right)\frac{2N}{L+\log L}

and using (
6.3
), we deduce that there exists a quantity
\eta(\lambda,L)
which is jointly continuous in
\lambda
and
L
with
\eta(\lambda,L)\to 0
as
L\to\infty
uniformly over
\lambda\in[\mu_{0}/\xi,3\mu_{0}/\xi]
, such that

1-\mathbb{E}_{e^{-\xi}R,R}\left[\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L)}{L}\right)\frac{2N}{L+\log L}\right\}\right]=-\frac{2e^{\pm\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}C_{\lambda}}

for every
\lambda\in[\mu_{0}/\xi,3\mu_{0}/\xi]
.
Starting now from a point on the sphere
\partial B(e^{-x_{0}}R)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[1-\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L)}{L}\right)\frac{2N}{L+\log L}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-\xi}R}=n\right)\left(1-\mathbb{E}_{e^{-\xi}R,R}\left[\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L)}{L}\right)\frac{2N}{L+\log L}\right\}\right]^{n}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-\xi}R}=n\right)\left(1-\left(1+\frac{2e^{\pm\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}C_{\lambda}}\right)^{n}\right),

where
e^{\pm\delta}
may represent a different quantity bounded between
e^{-\delta}
and
e^{\delta}
in each term of the sum.
The linearisation
(1+u)^{n}\approx 1+nu
is a good approximation as long as
nu
is small. Using the estimate (
6.3
) on
C_{\lambda}
and recalling that
A=1/\mu_{0},\lambda=\mu/\xi
, we deduce after some elementary computations that for
n\leq n_{1}:=\delta(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}A\xi
,

1-\left(1+\frac{2e^{\pm\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}C_{\lambda}}\right)^{n}=-\frac{2e^{\pm 10\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}C_{\lambda}}n.

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[1-\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L)}{L}\right)\frac{2N}{L+\log L}\right\}\right]

(6.5)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt=-e^{\pm 10\delta}\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{e^{-\xi}R}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{N_{e^{-\xi}R}\leq n_{1}\right\}}\right]\frac{2}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}C_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\sum_{n=n_{1}+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-\xi}R}=n\right)\left(1-\left(1+\frac{2e^{\pm\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}A\xi}\right)^{n}\right).

It remains to argue that the contribution of the sum above is small and that the restriction to the event
\{N_{e^{-\xi}R}\leq n_{1}\}
leaves the expectation of
N_{e^{-\xi}R}
almost unchanged. This follows from (
6.4
): for all
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{n=kn_{1}+1}^{(k+1)n_{1}}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-\xi}R}=n\right)\left(1-\left(1+\frac{2e^{\pm\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}A\xi}\right)^{n}\right)\Big|

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-\xi}R}\geq kn_{1}+1\right)\left(1+\frac{2e^{\pm\delta}}{(e^{-\xi}R)^{2}A\xi}\right)^{(k+1)n_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leq\frac{C(x_{0})}{\xi(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}\exp\left(-k\delta A+2(k+1)e^{\pm 10\delta}\delta\right)\leq\frac{C(x_{0})}{\xi(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}\exp\left(-k\delta A/2\right)

since
A\geq 8e^{10\delta}
. This proves that the sum on the right hand side of (
6.5
) is at most

\frac{C^{\prime}(x_{0})}{\xi(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}}\frac{1}{\delta A}\exp\left(-\delta A/2\right)\leq\frac{\delta C^{\prime}(x_{0})}{(e^{-x_{0}}R)^{2}C_{\lambda}},

where we used (
6.2
) and the fact that
C_{\lambda}
is of order
A\xi
(see (
6.3
)) in the last inequality.
Similarly, one can show that the expectation on the right hand side of (
6.5
) equals
e^{\pm 10\delta}\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{e^{-\xi}R}\right]
.
The expectation
\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[N_{e^{-\xi}R}\right]
is equal to the probability that a Brownian trajectory starting on the sphere
\partial B(e^{-x_{0}}R)
hits
\partial B(e^{-\xi}R)
before
\partial B(R)
which is equal to

\frac{e^{2x_{0}}-1}{e^{2\xi}-1}=e^{\pm\delta}e^{-2\xi}(e^{2x_{0}}-1),

by (
6.4
).
Wrapping things up, we have obtained that

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[1-\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L)}{L}\right)\frac{2N}{L+\log L}\right\}\right]=-(1\pm C^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})\delta)\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}C_{\lambda}}.

By continuity of
\lambda\mapsto C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L)
for each fixed
L
, the intermediate value theorem and (
6.3
),
\{C_{\lambda}+\eta(\lambda,L),\lambda\in[A/\xi,3A/\xi]\}
contains the interval
[T_{0},2T_{0}]
with
T_{0}=C_{2.99A/\xi}
if
L
is large enough. The above estimate thus implies the claim.
∎

6.2
A finitary Tauberian theorem

In this section we prove the following finitary Tauberian theorem, which will be applied when we deduce Theorem
1.3
from Theorem
6.1
.

Theorem 6.3
.

For each
0<a<1
and
0<c<C<\infty
there exists
\delta>0
such that if
T>1
and
X
is a non-negative random variable satisfying

\frac{e^{-\delta}}{1-\lambda}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda X}\right]\leq\frac{e^{\delta}}{1-\lambda}

for every
1-C/T\leq\lambda\leq 1-c/T
then

\mathbb{P}\Bigl((1-a)T\leq X\leq(1+a)T\Bigr)\geq\delta aT\exp\left[-(1+a)T\right].

Remark 6.4
.

The proof of this theorem also yields an upper bound of the same form. We focus on the lower bound since upper bounds on the tail are easy to obtain from generating function estimates via Markov’s inequality.

We begin the proof of this theorem with the following lemma.

Lemma 6.5
.

For each
0<\varepsilon<a<1
,
0<c_{1}<c_{2}<1
, and
A<\infty
there exists
\delta>0
such that if
\mu
is a probability measure on
[0,\infty)
satisfying

\frac{e^{-\delta}}{1-\lambda}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\lambda x}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\leq\frac{e^{\delta}}{1-\lambda}

for every
c_{1}\leq\lambda\leq c_{2}
then

\mu([y-a,y+a])\geq(1-\varepsilon)(1-e^{-2a})e^{-y+a}

for every
0\leq y\leq A
.

Note that
e^{-y+a}(1-e^{-2a})
is equal to the probability that an Exponential
(1)
random variable belongs to the interval
[y-a,y+a]
.

Proof of Lemma
6.5
.

Suppose not. Then there exists
0<\varepsilon<a<1
,
0<c_{1},c_{2}<1
,
A<\infty
, a sequence of measures
(\mu_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and a sequence of numbers
(y_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
[0,A]
such that

\frac{e^{-1/n}}{1-\lambda}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\lambda x}\mathrm{d}\mu_{n}(x)\leq\frac{e^{1/n}}{1-\lambda}

for every
n\geq 1
and
c_{1}\leq\lambda\leq c_{2}
but for which
\mu_{n}([y_{n}-a,y_{n}+a])\leq(1-\varepsilon)(1-e^{-2a})e^{-y_{n}+a}
for each
n\geq 1
. Taking a subsequence if necessary, we may take
y_{n}
to converge to a limit
y\in[0,A]
. Letting
0<\eta<1
be such that
(1-e^{-2(a-\eta)})e^{-y+a-\eta}>(1-\varepsilon)(1-e^{-2a})e^{-y+a-\varepsilon}
, it follows that
\mu_{n}([y-a+\eta,y+a-\eta])\leq\mu_{n}([y_{n}-a,y_{n}+a])\leq(1-\varepsilon)(1-e^{-2a})e^{-y_{n}+a}
for all sufficiently large
n
.
If
\mu
is the law of an Exp(1) random variable then
\mu([y-a+\eta,y+a-\eta])=(1-e^{-2(a-\eta)})e^{-y+a-\eta}>(1-\varepsilon)(1-e^{-2a})e^{-y+a-\varepsilon}
, so to reach a contradiction it suffices to prove that
\mu_{n}
converges weakly to
\mu
as
n\to\infty
; this is clear since the moment generating function of any subsequential limit of the
\mu_{n}
’s agrees with the moment generating function of
\mu
on the interval
[c_{1},c_{2}]
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

We will prove the theorem by applying Lemma
6.5
to a certain biased and rescaled version of
X
.
Fix
T>1
and suppose that
X
satisfies

\frac{e^{-\delta}}{1-\lambda}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda X}\right]\leq\frac{e^{\delta}}{1-\lambda}

for every
1-C/T\leq\lambda\leq 1-c/T
. Let
s=1-(c+C)/2T
and let
Z_{s}
be a random variable whose law is obtained by biasing the law of
X
by
e^{sX}
and multiplying the resulting random variable by
(1-s)
. The moment generating function of
Z_{s}
satisfies

\frac{e^{-2\delta}}{1-\lambda}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda Z_{s}}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{sX+(1-s)\lambda X}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{sX}\right]}\leq\frac{e^{2\delta}}{1-\lambda}

for every
\lambda
satisfying
1-C/T\leq s+(1-s)\lambda\leq 1-c/T
. This condition holds if and only if

1-2C/(c+C)\leq\lambda\leq 1-2c/(c+C)
. Thus, it follows from Lemma
6.5
(applied with “
y
” equal to
T
, “
a
” equal to
(1-s)Ta/2=(c+C)a/4
, and “
\varepsilon
” equal to
1/2
) that if
\delta>0
is sufficiently small as a function of
a
,
c
, and
C
then

\mathbb{P}\left(1-a\leq\frac{Z_{s}}{(1-s)T}\leq 1+a\right)\geq(1-\varepsilon)\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{(1-s)Ta}{2}\right)\right)e^{-1+a}

and hence that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((1-a)T\leq X\leq(1+a)T\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[e^{sX}]\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\frac{sZ_{s}}{1-s}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{1-a\leq\frac{Z_{s}}{(1-s)T}\leq 1+a\right\}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\frac{e^{-\delta}}{1-s}\left(1-\exp\left(-\frac{(1-s)Ta}{2}\right)\right)\exp\left[-s(1+a)T-1+a\right].

Since
s\leq 1
, the claim follows by using the approximation
1-\exp[-\frac{1}{2}(1-s)Ta]\geq c^{\prime}(1-s)Ta
(where
c^{\prime}>0
depends only on
c
and
C
) and noting that the two factors of
1-s
cancel.
∎

6.3
Proof of Theorem
1.3

This section combines the estimates on the Laplace transform (Theorem
6.1
) together with the Tauberian theorem from Section
6.2
to prove Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. We apply Theorem
6.3
with
{a=\varepsilon}
,
c=1
, and
C=2
, letting
\delta>0
be small enough that the statement made in Theorem
6.3
holds. Now, by Theorem
6.1
, there exists
u_{0}>0
(that we referred to as
T_{0}
in Theorem
6.1
),
R_{0}>0
and
x_{*}>x_{0}
large enough such that for all
u_{0}<u<2u_{0}
,
R>R_{0}
,
x>x_{*}
,

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[1-\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{u}{x}\right)\frac{2N_{e^{-x}R}}{(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right\}\right]=-e^{\pm\delta/3}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}x}\frac{x}{u},

where, as before, we write
e^{\pm\delta/3}
for a quantity bounded between
e^{-\delta/3}
and
e^{\delta/3}
whose precise value may vary from line to line.
Also, by Lemma
4.1
, if
x
and
R
are large enough,

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-x}R}>0\right)=e^{\pm\delta/3}\frac{2(e^{2x_{0}}-1)}{R^{2}x}.

(6.6)

These two estimates together imply that

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\left.\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{u}{x}\right)\frac{2N_{e^{-x}R}}{(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right\}\right|N_{e^{-x}R}>0\right]=1+e^{\pm 2\delta/3}\frac{x}{u}.

Fix
u\in[u_{0},2u_{0}]
. If we take
x
to be large enough that the first term on the right hand side is much smaller than the second one, we obtain that

\mathbb{E}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left[\left.\exp\left\{\left(1-\frac{u}{x}\right)\frac{2N_{e^{-x}R}}{(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}}\right\}\right|N_{e^{-x}R}>0\right]=e^{\pm\delta}\frac{x}{u}

whenever
x
and
R
are sufficiently large.
Applying Theorem
6.3
to
T=(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}x/u_{0}
and the random variable
X=\frac{2N_{e^{-x}R}}{(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}}
conditioned to be positive, we obtain that

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(\left.N_{e^{-x}R}\geq\frac{1}{2}\frac{x}{u_{0}}(x+\log x)(e^{-x}R)^{2}\right|N_{e^{-x}R}>0\right)\geq\frac{\delta\varepsilon x}{(1-\varepsilon)u_{0}}\exp\left[-\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{x}{u_{0}}\right]

Using (
6.6
) to rewrite this estimate slightly, we have shown for each
\varepsilon>0
that there exists
\delta^{\prime}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{e^{-x}R}\geq\frac{x(e^{-x}R)^{2}}{2}\frac{x}{u_{0}}\right)\geq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{R^{2}x}\exp\left[-\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{x}{u_{0}}\right]

(6.7)

for all
u_{0}>0
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
) and for all
x>x_{0}
large enough (depending on
\varepsilon
and
u_{0}
).
This shows that the lower bound of the desired form holds
for all sufficiently small values of
a
.

We now bootstrap this result to obtain any thickness level
a>0
instead of small levels
1/u_{0}
as follows.
Let
x_{0}^{\prime}=x_{0}+1
be an intermediate level.
Starting from a single particle on the sphere of radius
e^{-x_{0}}R
, we can first condition on the event that the branching process emanating from this particle contains at least
\left\lceil au_{0}\right\rceil(e^{-x_{0}^{\prime}}R)^{2}
pioneers on the sphere of radius
e^{-x_{0}^{\prime}}R
. This occurs with some probability larger than
c(a,u_{0},x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime})R^{-2}
. Conditionally on this event, to create at least
ax^{2}(e^{-x}R)^{2}/2
pioneers on the sphere
\partial B(e^{-x}R)
, we can ask each of the
\left\lceil au_{0}\right\rceil
sets of
(e^{-x_{0}^{\prime}}R)^{2}
pioneers on
\partial B(e^{-x_{0}^{\prime}}R)
to create at least
\frac{1}{u_{0}}x^{2}(e^{-x}R)^{2}/2
pioneers. By (
6.7
) applied to
x_{0}^{\prime}
instead of
x_{0}
, this probability is at least

\left(\frac{C}{x}e^{-(1+\varepsilon)x/u_{0}}\right)^{\left\lceil au_{0}\right\rceil}\geq e^{-(1+2\varepsilon)\frac{\left\lceil au_{0}\right\rceil}{u_{0}}x}.

Because
\varepsilon
and
u_{0}
can be chosen arbitrarily small and large respectively, this concludes the proof.
∎

7
Tail estimates without killing (proof of Theorem
1.2
)

In this we complete the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Since we have already proved above Theorem
1.3
, which implies directly the part of Theorem
1.2
with killing, it suffices to prove
(
1.3
), which is the part of that concerns the process in infinite volume, with no killing. In fact we will prove the following more general theorem which allows for a killing boundary at an arbitrary distance.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
x_{0}>0
and consider a thickness level
a>0
. For each
s\in[1,\infty]
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R^{s}}\left(\left.N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right|N_{1}>0\right)

\displaystyle=R^{-\psi_{s}(a)+o(1)}

(7.1)

as
R\to\infty
, where we set
\psi_{s}(a):=\inf_{1\leq t\leq s}(2(t-1)+at^{-1})
(note that
s=\infty
is allowed, in which case
\psi_{s}(a)
coincides with the function
\psi
in Theorem
1.2
).

To this end, we first state and prove the following intermediate result:

Lemma 7.2
.

For each fixed
t>1
and
a>0
, the estimate

\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{t}}\left(\mathrm{hit}~\partial B(R^{t})\text{ and }N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right)=R^{-2t-a/t+o(1)}

(7.2)

holds as
R\to\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

The lower bound follows quickly from the estimate (
1.4
) of Theorem
1.2
, concerning the process with killing on scale
R
. Indeed, starting with one initial particle on
\partial B(R)
, the probability that it reaches
\partial B(R^{t}/2)
with
2R^{2t}
pioneers is equal to
R^{-2t+o(1)}
. We then divide these pioneers into two conditionally independent groups of
R^{2t}
particles. The probability that the first group reaches
\partial B(R^{t})
is of constant order. Meanwhile, by (
1.4
), the probability that the second group generates
\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}=\frac{a}{2t^{2}}(\log R^{t})^{2}
pioneers on
\partial B(1)
before reaching
\partial B(R^{t})
is equal to
(R^{t})^{-a/t^{2}+o(1)}=R^{-a/t+o(1)}
. Altogether, this shows that the left hand side of (
7.2
) is at least
R^{-2t-a/t+o(1)}
.

The upper bound requires more work. For this, we will need to rely again on the PDE satisfied by the Laplace transform.
Fix
u\in(0,1/t)
and let
s=-2u/\log R
.
By Theorem
3.1
and Lemma
4.5
, there exists a positive constant
C(u)
such that

1-\mathbb{E}_{R,R^{t}}\left[\left(1-s\right)^{N_{1}}\right]\geq-\frac{C(u)}{R^{2}\log R}.

(7.3)

Let us define for
x\in[0,t\log R]
,

\mathsf{g}(x):=(R^{t}e^{-x})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{R^{t}e^{-x},R^{t}}\left[1-(1-s)^{N_{1}}\right],\quad\tilde{\mathsf{g}}(x):=(R^{t}e^{-x})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{R^{t}e^{-x},R^{t}}\left[1-(1-s)^{N_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{N_{R^{t}}=0\right\}}\right].

We are interested in the function
\mathsf{f}:=\tilde{\mathsf{g}}-\mathsf{g}\geq 0
, which is given by

\mathsf{f}(x)=(R^{t}e^{-x})^{2}\mathbb{E}_{R^{t}e^{-x},R^{t}}\left[(1-s)^{N_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{N_{R^{t}}>0\right\}}\right].

The two functions

\mathsf{g}
and
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}
satisfy

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{g}^{\prime\prime}+2\mathsf{g}^{\prime}=\mathsf{g}^{2},\\
\mathsf{g}(0)=0,~\mathsf{g}(t\log R)=s,\end{array}\right.\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime\prime}+2\tilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathsf{g}}^{2},\\
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}(0)=1,~\tilde{\mathsf{g}}(t\log R)=s,\end{array}\right.

while
\mathsf{f}
satisfies the ODE
\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}+2\mathsf{f}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{g}+\tilde{\mathsf{g}})\mathsf{f}
.

Let
\eta>0
.
Since
\sup_{[0,\infty)}|g_{\lambda}|\leq\lambda/2
(Lemma
3.12
) and the slope at the origin of
\mathsf{g}
goes to zero as
R\to\infty
(Lemma
4.5
), we have that

\mathsf{g}\geq-\eta/2
for all sufficiently large
R
. We now fix such a value of
R
and claim that

\mathsf{f}(x)\leq e^{-(2-\varepsilon)x},\quad\quad x\in[0,t\log R],\quad\quad\text{where}\quad\varepsilon=1-\sqrt{1-\eta}.

(7.4)

Once the claim (
7.4
) is proven, we may apply
this estimate with
x=(t-1)\log R
to deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{R,R^{t}}\left[(1+2u/\log R)^{N_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{N_{R^{t}}>0\right\}}\right]\leq R^{-2t+o(1)}

as
R\to\infty
(recalling that
u\in(0,1/t)
was fixed), from which the claimed upper bound of (
7.2
) may be deduced via Markov’s inequality and optimising over
u\in(0,1/t)
by taking
u
as close to
1/t
as desired.

We now prove the claimed inequality (
7.4
).
Since
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}\geq\mathsf{g}
, we have
\mathsf{g}+\tilde{\mathsf{g}}\geq-\eta
and

\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}+2\mathsf{f}^{\prime}\geq-\eta\mathsf{f}.

As in the proof of Lemma
3.11
, if we let
c_{\pm}=1\pm\sqrt{1-\eta}
we have that
c_{+}+c_{-}=2
and
c_{+}c_{-}=\eta
and hence that

(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}+c_{+}\mathsf{f})^{\prime}+c_{-}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}+c_{+}\mathsf{f})=\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}+2\mathsf{f}^{\prime}+\eta\mathsf{f}\geq 0.

Integrating between
x
and
t\log R
for some
x\in[0,t\log R]
(using Lemma
3.10
) leads to

(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(x)+c_{+}\mathsf{f}(x))e^{c_{-}x}\leq(\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(t\log R)+c_{+}\mathsf{f}(t\log R))e^{c_{-}t\log R}.

Because
\mathsf{f}(t\log R)=0
and
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}(t\log R)\leq 0
, this implies that
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}+c_{+}\mathsf{f}\leq 0
in
[0,t\log R]
.
Integrating further between
0
and some
x\in[0,\log R]
(using Lemma
3.10
a second time) leads to (
7.4
), which concludes the proof.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
7.1
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

Fix
a>0
and
s\in[1,\infty]
. The lower bound is a direct consequence of (
1.4
) when
a\leq 2
. If
a>2
, letting
t_{*}=\min\{s,\sqrt{a/2}\}
, we have by Lemma
7.2
that

\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{s}}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{t_{*}}}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},\mathrm{hit~}\partial B(R^{t_{*}})\right)=R^{-2t_{*}-a/t_{*}+o(1)}.

The exponent
t_{*}
was chosen to minimise the above exponent, which is equal to
\psi_{s}(a)
.
This concludes the proof of the lower bound.

For the upper bound, let
\varepsilon>0
be the inverse of a large integer, let
t_{k}=1+\varepsilon k
for each
k\geq 0
, and let
k_{\max}
be the largest integer such that

t_{k}\leq t_{*}=\min\{s,\sqrt{a/2}\}
.
Let
K:=\max\{k\geq 0:N_{R^{t_{k}}}>0\}
, where
N_{R^{t_{k}}}
is the number of pioneers on the large sphere
\partial B(R^{t_{k}})
when particles are frozen both on this sphere and the unit sphere.
We have by a union bound that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{s}}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{k_{\max}}\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{s}}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},K=k\right)+\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{s}}\left(K\geq k_{\max}+1\right).

If
t_{*}=s
then the last probability in the above display is equal to zero. Otherwise, this probability is

\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{s}}\left(N_{R^{t_{k_{\max}+1}}}>0\right)=R^{-2t_{k_{\max}+1}+o(1)}\leq R^{-2\sqrt{a/2}+o(1)}=R^{-2-\psi_{s}(a)+o(1)}

by definition of
k_{\max}
. In either case, we have that

\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{s}}\left(N_{R^{t_{k_{\max}+1}}}>0\right)\leq R^{-2-\psi_{s}(a)+o(1)}.

(7.5)

We now take
k\in\{0,\dots,k_{\max}\}
and work on the event
\{K=k\}
.
We can decompose
N_{1}
as
N_{1}=N_{1}^{(1)}+N_{1}^{(2)}
where
N_{1}^{(1)}
is the number of pioneers on
\partial B(1)
that
do not
have an ancestor in
\partial B(R^{t_{k}})
, and
N_{1}^{(2)}=N_{1}-N_{1}^{(1)}
is the number of pioneers on
\partial B(1)
that
do
have an ancestor in
\partial B(R^{t_{k}})
.
We have by a union bound that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{R,\infty}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},K=k\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{R,\infty}\left(N_{R^{t_{k}}}\geq R^{2t_{k}}(\log R)^{3/2}\right)

\displaystyle~~~~~~~~~~~~~~+\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{t_{k+1}}}\left(N_{1}^{(1)}+N_{1}^{(2)}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},0<N_{R^{t_{k}}}<R^{2t_{k}}(\log R)^{3/2}\right).

(7.6)

By Lemma
2.7
, the first probability on the right hand side decays faster than any polynomial in
R
.
Meanwhile, the second probability is at most

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{t_{k+1}}}\left(N_{1}^{(1)}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},0<N_{R^{t_{k}}}\right)+\sum_{n=1}^{1/\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{t_{k+1}}}\Big(N_{1}^{(1)}\in[1-n\varepsilon,1-(n-1)\varepsilon]\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},

\displaystyle N_{1}^{(2)}\geq\frac{(n-1)\varepsilon a}{2}(\log R)^{2},0<N_{R^{t_{k}}}<R^{2t_{k}}(\log R)^{3/2}\Big).

Conditionally on
N_{R^{t_{k}}}
, the random variables
N_{1}^{(1)}
and
N_{1}^{(2)}
are independent.
Moreover, by (
7.3
) and the conditional independence of particles after hitting
\partial B(R^{t_{k}})
, we have for all
v\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{E}_{R,R^{t_{k+1}}}\left[\left.\left(1+\frac{2v}{\log R^{t_{k+1}}}\right)^{N_{1}^{(2)}}\right|N_{R^{t_{k}}}<R^{2t_{k}}(\log R)^{3/2}\right]\leq\exp(C(v)(\log R)^{1/2})=R^{o(1)}.

By Markov’s inequality, this implies that, conditionally on
N_{R^{t_{k}}}<R^{2t_{k}}(\log R)^{3/2}
, the probability (for the branching Brownian motion with killing at
R^{t_{k+1}}
) that
N_{1}^{(2)}
is at least
\frac{(n-1)\varepsilon a}{2}(\log R)^{2}
is at most
R^{-(n-1)\varepsilon a/t_{k+1}+o(1)}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
7.2
,

\mathbb{P}_{R,R^{t_{k+1}}}\Big(N_{1}^{(1)}\geq(1-n\varepsilon)\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},0<N_{R^{t_{k}}}\Big)\leq R^{-2t_{k}-(1-n\varepsilon)a/t_{k}+o(1)}.

This shows that the second probability in the right hand side of (
7.6
) is at most

\displaystyle R^{-2t_{k}-a/t_{k}+o(1)}+\sum_{n=1}^{1/\varepsilon}R^{-2t_{k}-(1-n\varepsilon)a/t_{k}-(n-1)\varepsilon a/t_{k+1}+o(1)}\leq R^{-2t_{k}-a/t_{k}+2a\varepsilon+o(1)},

where in the last inequality we made elementary simplifications and, in particular, used the bound

\frac{1}{t_{k+1}}=\frac{1}{t_{k}+\varepsilon}\geq\frac{1}{t_{k}}\Big(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{t_{k}}\Big)\geq\frac{1}{t_{k}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{t_{k}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{t_{k}}-\varepsilon

(as
t_{k}\geq 1
).
Wrapping up, we have shown that

\mathbb{P}_{R,\infty}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},K=k\right)\leq R^{-2t_{k}-a/t_{k}+2a\varepsilon+o(1)}

for each
0\leq k\leq k_{\mathrm{max}}

and hence that

\mathbb{P}_{R,\infty}\left(N_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},K\leq k_{\mathrm{max}}\right)\leq k_{\mathrm{max}}\cdot R^{-\min_{0\leq k\leq k_{\mathrm{max}}}(2t_{k}+at_{k}^{-1})+2a\varepsilon+o(1)}\leq R^{-\psi_{s}(a)+2a\varepsilon+o(1)}.

(7.7)

The claimed upper bound follows from (
7.5
) and (
7.7
) since
\varepsilon>0
was arbitrary.
∎

8
Thick points (proof of Theorem
1.5
)

In this section we apply the main results of Sections
4
and
6
to prove Theorem
1.5
.
The upper bound in this theorem has already been proved at the end of Section
4.3
,
so that it remains only to prove the (more difficult) lower bound.

8.1
Strategy of the proof

We now explain the overall strategy behind the proof of the lower bound of (
1.11
) in Theorem
1.5
. In particular, we will state three key propositions (Propositions
8.1
,
8.2
and
8.3
), and explain how these propositions imply Theorem
1.5
. The rest of Section
8
is then dedicated to the proofs of these three key propositions.

As is common in the study of analogous questions for other models, we use a truncated second moment approach. The truncation we use will be described in terms of
non-backtracking trajectories
, a notion we now introduce.
Let
\eta_{1}>0
be the inverse of a large integer, let
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
, and define
r_{k}=R^{1-k\eta_{1}}
for each
k\in\{1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}\}
.
We say that a pioneer in
\partial B(z,r)
is
non-backtracking
if, for each
1\leq k\leq 1/\eta_{1}
, the trajectory taken by the ancestors of the pioneer does not revisit the sphere
\partial B(z,r_{k}\log R)
after first visiting the sphere
\partial B(z,r_{k})
. (Note that this makes sense even if
r
is larger than some of the
r_{k}
.)
We denote the number of non-backtracking pioneers on the sphere
\partial B(z,r)
by
\hat{N}_{z,r}
, noting that
\hat{N}_{z,r}
depends implicitly on the choice of
\eta_{1}
and
R
.

In the first key proposition, we show that restricting the branching process to non-backtracking trajectories does not reduce the exponent for a single point to be thick
(as can be seen by comparing this result with Theorem
1.3
). The constants
1/2
and
1/10
appearing in this proposition are somewhat arbitrary.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
z,z_{0}\in B(0,R/2)
with
|z-z_{0}|\geq R/10
. We have

\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\hat{N}_{z,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right)\geq R^{-2-a+o(1)}\quad\quad\text{as}\quad R\to\infty.

The next key proposition will state that the exponent for a point to be thick remains the same if, in addition to restricting to non-backtracking trajectories, we also impose a “good event” stating that the number of non-backtracking pioneers on each mesoscopic scale is of order consistent with the desired level of thickness at the unit scale.
Let
b>a
be a parameter close to
a
and define the good event
G_{R}(z)
by

G_{R}(z):=\left\{\forall k=1,\dots,1/\eta_{1},\hat{N}_{z,r}\leq\frac{b}{2}r_{k}^{2}\left(\log\frac{R}{r_{k}}\right)^{2}\right\}.

(8.1)

The definition of this good event is inspired by the Gaussian and Brownian multiplicative chaos theories; see e.g.
[
Ber17
,
Jeg20a
]
.
In the next result, we show that adding this second layer of good event (the first layer being the restriction to non-backtracking particles) keeps the first moment almost unchanged (rather than merely keeping the exponent the same).

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
z_{0},z\in B(0,R)
with
|z-z_{0}|\geq R/10
. There exists
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(a,b,\eta_{1})>0
such that for all
R
large enough,

\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\hat{N}_{z,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},G_{R}(z)^{c}\right)\leq R^{-2-a-\varepsilon_{1}}.

(8.2)

Finally, we bound the relevant truncated two-point function, where the truncation both restricts to non-backtracking trajectories and imposes that the relevant good events hold.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\eta_{3}>0
be a small parameter,
z_{0}\in B(R/2)\setminus B(R/4)
and
z,w\in B(0,R^{1-\eta_{3}})
. There exists
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}(a,b,\eta_{1},\eta_{3})>0
such that

\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\hat{N}_{z,1},\hat{N}_{w,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},G_{R}(z),G_{R}(w)\right)\leq R^{-2-2a+o(1)}\left(\frac{R}{|z-w|}\right)^{a+\varepsilon_{2}}.

(8.3)

Moreover, for
a
and
\eta_{3}
fixed,
\varepsilon_{2}(a,b,\eta_{1},\eta_{3})\to 0
as
b\to a^{+}
and
\eta_{1}\to 0
.

Assuming the three propositions above, we are now ready to prove the lower bound in Theorem
1.5
. In a nutshell, an application of Paley–Zygmund inequality will show that the probability of having a large number of thick points decays slower than any power of
R
. We will then take advantage of the numerous independent branching Brownian motions present in a branching Brownian motion conditioned to hit
\partial B(R/2)
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
– Lower bound.

Without loss of generality, we change the conditioning: rather than conditioning on surviving at least
R^{2}
generations, we will condition on the event that at least one particle reaches the outer sphere
\partial B(R)
. We will denote by
\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}
this conditional law. (Any conditioning that guarantees that the BGW tree survives at least
R^{2+o(1)}
generations with high probability would do.)
Note that
it suffices to prove (
1.11
), since this trivially implies the lower bound on the maximal thickness.
Let
\eta_{3}>0
be as in Proposition
8.3
(small, but much larger than
\eta_{1}
) and define the following subset of
\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)
:

\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a):=\left\{z\in B(0,R^{1-\eta_{3}})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{4}:\hat{N}_{z,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\right\}.

Let
\delta>0
. By Propositions
8.1
and
8.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{0},R}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right]\geq R^{-2+4(1-\eta_{3})-a+o(1)}.

(8.4)

Under
\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}
there is at least one particle hitting
\partial B(R)
. Pick such a particle uniformly at random, and let
E
be the event that the ancestors of this particle gave birth to at least
R^{2}
particles, each starting in
B(R/2)\setminus B(R/4)
.
It is easy to see that the conditional probability of
E
is uniformly bounded away from 0:
\inf_{R}\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}^{*}(E)>0
, and that conditionally on
E
under
\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}
, and conditionally on the location of these branching events, the descendants form
R^{2}
independent branching Brownian motions starting from the given locations in
B(R/2)\setminus B(R/4)
. We deduce from (
8.4
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{0},R}^{*}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}^{*}(E)R^{2}\inf_{z_{0}^{\prime}\in B(R/2)\setminus B(R/4)}\mathbb{E}_{z_{0}^{\prime},R}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq R^{4(1-\eta_{3})-a+o(1)}.

Meanwhile, by Proposition
8.3
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{0},R}\left[\left(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right)^{2}\right]\leq R^{-2-2a+o(1)}\sum_{z,w\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}\cap B(0,R^{1-\eta_{3}})}\left(\frac{R}{|z-w|}\right)^{a+\varepsilon_{2}}.

If
b
is close enough to
a
and
\eta_{1}
is sufficiently small, then
a+\varepsilon_{2}<4
, so that the above sum can be bounded by
O(R^{8(1-\eta_{3})+\eta_{3}(a+\varepsilon_{2})})
and the above second moment is bounded above by
R^{-2+8(1-\eta_{3})-2a+\eta_{3}(a+\varepsilon_{2})+o(1)}.
To deduce a similar bound regarding the second moment under the conditioned probability
\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}^{*}
, we simply use the fact that the probability of hitting
\partial B(R)
is of order
R^{2}
so that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{z_{0},R}^{*}\Bigg[\Big(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\Big)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\text{hit~}\partial B(R)\right)}\mathbb{E}_{z_{0},R}\Bigg[\Big(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\Big)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq R^{8(1-\eta_{3})-2a+\eta_{3}(a+\varepsilon_{2})+o(1)}.

Using the Paley–Zygmund inequality, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left(\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\geq R^{4(1-\eta_{3})-a-\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\geq\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\geq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left[\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right]^{2}\left/\mathbb{E}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left[\left(\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{z\in\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{R}(a)\right\}}\mathbf{1}_{G_{R}(z)}\right)^{2}\right]\right.\geq R^{-\eta_{3}(a+\varepsilon_{2})-o(1)}.

Rephrasing slightly, we have proved that for any
\delta>0
,

\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left(\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\geq R^{4-a-\delta}\right)\geq R^{-o(1)}.

(8.5)

Using again the fact that
\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\text{hit~}\partial B(R)\right)=R^{-2+o(1)}
, this implies the following estimate for the unconditioned probability: for any
\delta>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\geq R^{4-a-\delta}\right)\geq R^{-2+o(1)}.

(8.6)

To conclude the proof, it remains to replace the
R^{o(1)}
on the right hand side of (
8.5
) by some quantity that goes to 1 as
R\to\infty
.
Let
\delta>0
be small and let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough so that
(4-a-\delta)/(1-\varepsilon)<4-a
.
Let
E
be the event that for all
k=\left\lfloor R^{\varepsilon}/4\right\rfloor,\left\lfloor R^{\varepsilon}/4\right\rfloor+1,\dots,\left\lfloor R^{\varepsilon}/2\right\rfloor-1
, the number of pioneers on the sphere
\partial B(R/2+kR^{1-\varepsilon})
is at least
R^{2-\varepsilon/2}
. For each
k
and each such pioneer, consider the number of thick points generated by that pioneer where particles are killed on the next sphere
\partial B(R/2+(k+1)R^{1-\varepsilon})
. These numbers of thick points stochastically dominate
\#\mathcal{T}_{2R^{1-\varepsilon}}(a)
under
\mathbb{P}_{w_{0},2R^{1-\varepsilon}}
, where
w_{0}\in\partial B(R^{1-\varepsilon})
.
If
\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\leq R^{4-a-\delta}
, then all these subsets of
\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)
contain at most
R^{4-a-\delta}=(R^{1-\varepsilon})^{(4-a-\delta)/(1-\varepsilon)}
elements (recall that
(4-a-\delta)/(1-\varepsilon)<4-a
). On the event
E
, there are at least
cR^{\varepsilon}R^{2-\varepsilon/2}=cR^{2+\varepsilon/2}
of these subsets. By a repeated application of the Markov property and by (
8.6
) applied to
2R^{1-\varepsilon}
and to a starting point on
\partial B(R^{1-\varepsilon})
instead of
R
and
z_{0}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}\left(E,\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\leq R^{4-a-\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R^{2+o(1)}\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(E,\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\leq R^{4-a-\delta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R^{2+o(1)}(1-R^{-2+o(1)})^{cR^{2+\varepsilon/2}}=R^{2+o(1)}e^{-R^{\varepsilon/2+o(1)}}\to 0

as
R\to\infty
.
Together with the fact that
\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}(E)\to 1
as
R\to\infty
, this concludes the proof of the fact that
\mathbb{P}^{*}_{z_{0},R}(\#\mathcal{T}_{R}(a)\leq R^{4-a-\delta})\to 0
as
R\to\infty
.
∎

8.2
One-point estimate in a non-backtracking scenario

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
8.1
. We start with a preliminary lemma, which is essentially just a restatement of some of our earlier results.
Recall that
\eta_{1}
stands for the inverse of a large integer and that the decreasing sequence of radii
(r_{k})_{k}
is defined by
r_{k}=R^{1-k\eta_{1}}
, for
k=1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\eta_{1}>0
be the inverse of an integer. Let
[\nu_{-},\nu_{+}]\subset(0,1)
be a compact interval of
(0,1)
.
For all
\nu\in[\nu_{-},\nu_{+}]
and
k\in\{1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}\}
,

\mathbb{E}_{r_{k},r_{k}\log R}\left[\exp\left(\frac{2\nu}{r_{k+1}^{2}\log\frac{r_{k}}{r_{k+1}}}N_{r_{k+1}}\right)\right]=1+(1+o(1))\frac{2}{r_{k}^{2}\log(r_{k}/r_{k+1})}\frac{\nu}{1-\nu}

(8.7)

where
o(1)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
, uniformly in
\nu\in[\nu_{-},\nu_{+}]
and
k\in\{1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}\}
.

Proof.

We will use the function
g_{\lambda}
as defined in (
3.1
) and studied extensively in Sections
3
,
4
, and
5
.
Let
\mu=4\nu/(1-\nu)
. Because
\nu
belongs to a compact subspace of
(0,1)
,
\mu
belongs to a compact subspace of
(0,\infty)
.
Letting
x_{0}=\log\log R
and
x=\eta_{1}\log R+\log\log R
, we have
r_{k}=(r_{k}\log R)e^{-x_{0}}
and
r_{k+1}=(r_{k}\log R)e^{-x}
and, by Theorem
3.1
, that

1-\mathbb{E}_{r_{k},r_{k}\log R}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{r_{k+1}^{2}}g_{\lambda}(x)\right)^{N_{r_{k+1}}}\right]=\frac{1}{r_{k}^{2}}g_{\lambda}(x_{0})

for all
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Taking
\lambda=\mu/x
, we have by Lemma
4.5
that

g_{\lambda}(x)=-(1+o(1))\frac{2\mu}{\mu+4}\frac{1}{x}=-(1+o(1))2\nu\frac{1}{\eta_{1}\log R}.

Also, by (
4.9
) and because
1\ll x_{0}\ll\frac{1}{\lambda}
, we have

g_{\lambda}(x_{0})=-(1+o(1))\frac{\lambda}{2}=(1+o(1))\frac{2\nu}{1-\nu}\frac{1}{\eta_{1}\log R}.

Putting things together, we obtain the desired claim.
∎

Let
\eta_{2}>0
be much smaller than
\eta_{1}
and define for all
k=1\dots 1/\eta_{1}
,
I_{k}=\frac{a}{2}(1-\eta_{2})^{k}r_{k}^{2}\log(\frac{R}{r_{k}})^{2}
. The main intermediate step in the proof of Proposition
8.1
is summarised by the following lemma.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
k\in\{1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}-1\}
. For each
i=1,\ldots,I_{k}
, let
N^{(i)}
be i.i.d. random variables distributed according to the number of pioneers on
\partial B(r_{k+1})
, starting from a single particle on
\partial B(r_{k})
and where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{k}\log R)
. We have

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}\right)\geq R^{-a\eta_{1}(1+O(\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}))}

where the implicit constants in
O(\eta_{2}/\eta_{1})
are uniform provided that
R
is sufficiently large.

Proof.

The idea of the proof can be easily explained with the following toy model. Let
n
be a large integer and
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}
be i.i.d. exponential variables with mean 1. We are interested in the probability that
E_{1}+\dots+E_{n}
exceeds
tn
, where
t
is some fixed real number larger than
1
. To achieve this atypical behaviour, an optimal strategy consists in shifting the mean of each individual exponential variable to
t
, i.e. to shift the probability measure by

t^{-n}e^{(1-1/t)(E_{1}+\dots+E_{n})}.

After this shift, the sum
E_{1}+\dots+E_{n}
exceeds
tn
with a fairly high probability (asymptotic to 1/2 by the central limit theorem, but more importantly larger than any exponential in
n
).
Since the numbers of pioneers behave in many ways like exponential random variables, we will be able to use such a strategy as well. Moreover, since we have a good control on the Laplace transform of the number of pioneers, we will be able to control the cost of such a shift.

We now define the change of measure that we will use in the proof. Let
k\in\{1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}\}
, let
\nu=1/(1+k)
,
let

t=\frac{2\nu}{r_{k+1}^{2}\log\frac{r_{k}}{r_{k+1}}}

and let
\mathbb{Q}
be the probability law

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}=\left.\exp\left(t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right/\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right].

By (
8.7
) and by the special choice of
\nu
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right]=\left(1+\frac{2(1+o(1))}{r_{k}^{2}\log(r_{k}/r_{k+1})}\frac{\nu}{1-\nu}\right)^{I_{k}}

(8.8)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\exp\left((1+o(1))(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\frac{\nu}{1-\nu}\log^{2}\left(\frac{R}{r_{k}}\right)\Big/\log\left(\frac{r_{k}}{r_{k-1}}\right)\right)=R^{(1+o(1))(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}k}.

Define
I_{k+1}^{+}=\frac{1+\eta_{2}}{1-\eta_{2}}I_{k+1}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left(I_{k+1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle=R^{(1+o(1))(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\left\{I_{k+1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}^{+}\right\}}\exp\left(-t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right]

When the sum of the
N^{(i)}
is smaller than
I_{k+1}^{+}
, we can bound from below the exponential in the expectation above by
\exp\left(-tI_{k+1}^{+}\right)
. This leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}\right)\geq R^{((1+o(1))(1-\eta_{2})^{k}k-(1-\eta_{2})^{k-1}(1+\eta_{2})(k+1))\eta_{1}a}\mathbb{Q}\left(I_{k+1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt=R^{-(1+O(\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}))(1+o(1))\eta_{1}a}\mathbb{Q}\left(I_{k+1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}^{+}\right).

To conclude the proof it is enough to show that the above probability appearing on the second line is equal to
(1+o(1))
. To do this, we will show separately that
\mathbb{Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}\right)
and
\mathbb{Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}^{+}\right)
vanish as
R\to\infty
. We start by bounding the first probability and we let
\mu=\nu+\frac{k}{k+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\eta_{2}}}-1
(which is positive and smaller than
\nu
). By Markov’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{\mu}{\nu}tI_{k+1}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{\nu}t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=R^{-(1+o(1))(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}k}R^{\mu(1-\eta_{2})^{k+1}(k+1)^{2}a\eta_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\nu-\mu}{\nu}t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right]

where the first power of
R
on the right hand side comes from the normalisation of the Radon–Nikodym derivative
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}/\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
; see (
8.8
).
By (
8.7
), this is further equal to

\displaystyle\exp\left[(1+o(1))\left(-(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}k+\mu(1-\eta_{2})^{k+1}(k+1)^{2}a\eta_{1}+(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}\frac{\nu-\mu}{1-(\nu-\mu)}k^{2}\right)\log R\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[(1+o(1))a\eta_{1}(1-\eta_{2})^{k}\left(-k+\mu(1-\eta_{2})(k+1)^{2}+\frac{\nu-\mu}{1-(\nu-\mu)}k^{2}\right)\log R\right].

Our specific choice for
\mu
leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\leq I_{k+1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left[-(1+o(1))a\eta_{1}(1-\eta_{2})^{k}(2\sqrt{1-\eta_{2}}-2+\eta_{2})k(k+1)\log R\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left[-\frac{1}{5}a\eta_{1}\eta_{2}^{2}k(k+1)\log R\right]\to 0

as
R\to\infty
. To bound the other probability, we proceed similarly. We define this time
\mu=\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\eta_{2}}}\right)\frac{k}{k+1}
(which is positive and smaller than
1-\nu
). By Markov’s inequality and (
8.7
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}^{+}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\mu}{\nu}tI_{k+1}^{+}\right)\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\mu}{\nu}t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=R^{-(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}k}R^{-\mu(1-\eta_{2})^{k}(1+\eta_{2})(k+1)^{2}a\eta_{1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\nu+\mu}{\nu}t\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\left(-(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}k-\mu(1-\eta_{2})^{k}(1+\eta_{2})(k+1)^{2}a\eta_{1}+(1-\eta_{2})^{k}a\eta_{1}\frac{\nu+\mu}{1-(\nu+\mu)}k^{2}\right)\log R\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[{\left(a\eta_{1}(1-\eta_{2})^{k}\left(-k-\mu(1+\eta_{2})(k+1)^{2}+\frac{\nu+\mu}{1-(\nu+\mu)}k^{2}\right)\right)}\log R\right].

Our choice for
\mu
leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}^{+}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left[-a\eta_{1}(1-\eta_{2})^{k}\left(2+\eta_{2}-2\sqrt{1+\eta_{2}}\right)k(k+1)\log R\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left[-\frac{1}{5}a\eta_{1}\eta_{2}^{2}k(k+1)\log R\right]\to 0

as
R\to\infty
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
8.5
.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
8.1
.

Proof of Proposition
8.1
.

With the same notations as in Lemma
8.5
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(\hat{N}_{1}\geq I_{1/\eta_{1}}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r_{1}}\geq I_{1}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{1/\eta_{1}-1}\mathbb{P}_{r_{k},r_{k}\log R}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I_{k}}N_{r_{k+1}}^{(i)}\geq I_{k+1}\right).

By Theorem
1.3
, the first probability on the right hand side is at least

R^{-2-a\eta_{1}(1-\eta_{2})+o(1)}
. By Lemma
8.5
, each probability appearing in the product is at least
R^{-a\eta_{1}(1+O(\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}))}
. Using the definition of
I_{1/\eta_{1}}
, this gives

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(\hat{N}_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(1-\eta_{2})^{1/\eta_{1}}(\log R)^{2}\right)\geq R^{-2-a(1+O(\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}))}.

Because
\eta_{2}
can be as small as desired, this concludes the proof.
∎

8.3
Truncated first moment

We now prove Proposition
8.2
, which states that we can add the good event
G_{R}(z)
to our non-backtracking thick point event without significantly changing its probability.

Proof of Proposition
8.2
.

Since the left hand side of (
8.2
) increases as the value of
b
approaches
a
, we can assume that
b
is close enough to
a
to ensure that
\sqrt{b}(1-\eta_{1})<\sqrt{a}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be small enough so that

\frac{a+\varepsilon}{b}\frac{a+\varepsilon}{a}<1-\varepsilon,\quad\sqrt{\frac{b}{a}}(1-\eta_{1})<1-\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\eta_{1}^{2}(\sqrt{b}-\sqrt{a})^{2}>\varepsilon.

Now let
\delta>0
be small enough such that for all
p\geq 0
,

\frac{1+(p+1)\delta}{(1+p\delta)^{2}}\frac{a+\varepsilon}{b}\frac{a+\varepsilon}{a}<1-\varepsilon,\quad\sqrt{\frac{b}{a}(1+\delta)}(1-\eta_{1})<1-\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\eta_{1}(-\delta b+\eta_{1}(\sqrt{b}-\sqrt{a})^{2})>\varepsilon.

(8.9)

Let
J_{k}=\frac{b}{2}r_{k}^{2}\left(\log\frac{R}{r_{k}}\right)^{2}
.
By a union bound, the left hand side of (
8.2
) is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{1/\eta_{1}-1}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(\hat{N}_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},\hat{N}_{r_{k}}\geq J_{k}\right).

Fix
k\in\{1,\dots,1/\eta_{1}-1\}
. Let
N^{(i)}_{1},i\geq 1
, be i.i.d. random variables having the same law as the number of pioneers on the unit sphere with one initial particle on
\partial B(r_{k})
and where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{k}\log R)
. Conditionally on
\hat{N}_{r_{k}}=n
, the random variable
\hat{N}_{1}
is stochastically dominated by
\sum_{i=1}^{n}N_{1}^{(i)}
(there are additional killings in the variable
\hat{N}_{1}
, so that their distributions are not identical). Therefore, we can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(\hat{N}_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},\hat{N}_{r_{k}}\geq J_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(1+p\delta\leq\frac{\hat{N}_{r_{k}}}{J_{k}}<1+(p+1)\delta\right)\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{(1+(p+1)\delta)J_{k}}N_{1}^{(i)}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\Bigg).

(8.10)

By Proposition
4.6
, the first probability on the right hand side is at most

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r_{k}}\geq(1+p\delta)J_{k}\right)\leq R^{-2}e^{-(1+o(1))(1+p\delta)b\log\frac{R}{r_{k}}}=R^{-2-(1+o(1))(1+p\delta)b\eta_{1}k}.

When
(1+p\delta)b\eta_{1}k\geq a+\varepsilon
, we simply bound the second probability in (
8.10
) by 1. The contribution of the sum over
p
large enough so that
(1+p\delta)b\eta_{1}k\geq a+\varepsilon
is then at most
R^{-2-a-\varepsilon+o(1)}
. We now control the remaining part of the sum and take
p\geq 0
such that
(1+p\delta)b\eta_{1}k<a+\varepsilon
.
Let

\nu=1-\sqrt{\frac{b}{a}(1+(p+1)\delta)}\eta_{1}k.

The first two inequalities in (
8.9
) ensure that
\nu\in(\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon)
. Applying Markov’s inequality and recalling that
r_{k}=R^{1-k\eta_{1}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{(1+(p+1)\delta)J_{k}}N_{1}^{(i)}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\Bigg)\leq R^{-\frac{a\nu}{1-k\eta_{1}}}\mathbb{E}_{r_{k},r_{k}\log R}\left[\exp\left(\frac{2\nu N_{1}}{\log r_{k}}\right)\right]^{(1+(p+1)\delta)J_{k}}.

Similarly to (
8.7
),

\mathbb{E}_{r_{k},r_{k}\log R}\left[\exp\left(\frac{2\nu N_{1}}{\log r_{k}}\right)\right]=1+(1+o(1))\frac{2}{r_{k}^{2}\log r_{k}}\frac{\nu}{1-\nu}.

After simplifications, this leads to

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{(1+(p+1)\delta)J_{k}}N_{1}^{(i)}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\Bigg)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1+o(1)}{1-k\eta_{1}}\left(\sqrt{(1+(p+1)\delta)b}\eta_{1}k-\sqrt{a}\right)^{2}\log R\right).

(8.11)

Overall this gives that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r_{k}}\geq(1+p\delta)J_{k}\right)\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{(1+(p+1)\delta)J_{k}}N_{1}^{(i)}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq R^{-2}\exp\left(-(1+o(1))\left((1+p\delta)b\eta_{1}k+\frac{1}{1-k\eta_{1}}\left(\sqrt{(1+(p+1)\delta)b}\eta_{1}k-\sqrt{a}\right)^{2}\right)\log R\right)

\displaystyle=R^{-2}\exp\left(-(1+o(1))\left(a-\delta b\eta_{1}k+\frac{\eta_{1}k}{1-\eta_{1}k}\left(\sqrt{(1+(p+1)\delta)b}-\sqrt{a}\right)^{2}\right)\log R\right).

By the last inequality in (
8.9
),
we can bound from below the exponent

-\delta b\eta_{1}k+\frac{\eta_{1}k}{1-\eta_{1}k}\left(\sqrt{(1+(p+1)\delta)b}-\sqrt{a}\right)^{2}\geq\eta_{1}(-\delta b+\eta_{1}(\sqrt{b}-\sqrt{a})^{2})>\varepsilon.

We have shown that when
p
is small enough so that
(1+p\delta)b\eta_{1}k<a+\varepsilon
, the probability in (
8.10
) is at most
R^{-2-a-\varepsilon+o(1)}
. Since we have already bounded the remaining contribution of the sum, this concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

We note that the appearance of a quadratic exponent in (
8.11
) after taking the square root of the thickness parameters
a
and
b
is highly suggestive of an isomorphism-type picture, similar to the isomorphism relating the occupation of random walk trajectories to square of Gaussian free field
[
LJ11
,
MR06
]
.

8.4
Truncated second moment

We now prove Proposition
8.3
, which upper bounds the probability for two points to be thick points under the nonbacktracking condition, and each satisfying the good event.

Proof of Proposition
8.3
.

Let
z,w\in B(0,R^{1-\eta_{3}})
, and recall that
\eta_{3}
is fixed while
\eta_{1}\to 0
. If
2|z-w|\leq R^{\eta_{1}}\log R
, then we can simply bound the left hand side of (
8.3
) by the probability that a single point is thick which is at most
R^{-2-a+o(1)}

and so (after elementary algebra) this probability is smaller than the right hand side of (
8.3
) with
\varepsilon_{2}=a\eta_{1}/(1+\eta_{1})
.

Thus we suppose now that
2|z-w|\geq R^{\eta_{1}}\log R
, hence we can find
k\in\{k_{0},\dots,1/\eta_{1}-1\}
such that

r_{k}\log R\leq 2|z-w|\leq r_{k-1}\log R.

(8.12)

Here
k_{0}=k_{0}(\eta_{3},\eta_{1})
can be chosen arbitrarily large if
\eta_{1}
is small enough for any given
\eta_{3}>0
. By the definition of the good event, the left hand side of (
8.3
) is at most

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\Big(\hat{N}_{z,1},\hat{N}_{w,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},\hat{N}_{z,r_{k}},\hat{N}_{w,r_{k}}\leq\frac{b}{2}r_{k}^{2}\Big(\log\frac{R}{r_{k}}\Big)^{2}\Big).

Let
\delta>0
be the inverse of a large integer and denote by
J_{k}=\frac{b}{2}r_{k}^{2}\left(\log\frac{R}{r_{k}}\right)^{2}
. The above probability is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq p_{1},p_{2}<1/\delta}\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\Big(\hat{N}_{z,1},\hat{N}_{w,1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2},p_{1}\delta<\frac{\hat{N}_{z,r_{k}}}{J_{k}}\leq(p_{1}+1)\delta,p_{2}\delta<\frac{\hat{N}_{w,r_{k}}}{J_{k}}\leq(p_{2}+1)\delta\Big).

Let
p_{1},p_{2}\in\{0,\dots,1/\delta-1\}
. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
p_{2}\geq p_{1}
. Using the nonbacktracking condition, the descendants of the particles counted in
\hat{N}_{z,r_{k}}
and
\hat{N}_{w,r_{k}}
respectively, and which contribute to
\hat{N}_{z,1}
and
\hat{N}_{w,1}
respectively, are independent from one another. Hence we may bound the probability appearing in the above sum by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(N_{w,r_{k}}>p_{2}\delta J_{k}\right)\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{(p_{j}+1)\delta J_{k}}N^{(i)}_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\Bigg)

(8.13)

where
N^{(i)}_{1},i\geq 1
, are i.i.d. random variables distributed according to the number of pioneers on the unit sphere starting from a single particle on
\partial B(r_{k})
and where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{k}\log R)
. By (
8.11
), for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{(p_{j}+1)\delta J_{k}}N^{(i)}_{1}\geq\frac{a}{2}(\log R)^{2}\Bigg)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{1+o(1)}{1-k\eta_{1}}\left(\sqrt{(p_{j}+1)\delta b}\eta_{1}k-\sqrt{a}\right)^{2}\log R\right)

and by Proposition
4.6
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{z_{0},R}\left(N_{w,r_{k}}>p_{2}\delta J_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R^{-2+o(1)}\exp\left(-bp_{2}\delta\log\frac{R}{r_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq R^{-2+o(1)-bp_{2}\delta\eta_{1}k}\leq R^{-2+o(1)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}bp_{j}\delta\eta_{1}k}.

In the last inequality above, we used the fact that
p_{2}\geq p_{1}
.
Putting things together, and combining terms suitably, (
8.13
) is at most

\displaystyle R^{-2-2a+o(1)}\exp\Bigg(-\frac{k\eta_{1}}{1-k\eta_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(a-2\sqrt{(p_{j}+1)\delta ab}+(p_{j}+1)\delta b\eta_{1}k+\frac{1-k\eta_{1}}{2}p_{j}\delta b\right)\log R\Bigg)

\displaystyle=R^{-2-2a+o(1)}\exp\Bigg(-\frac{k\eta_{1}}{1-k\eta_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\left(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{(p_{j}+1)\delta b}\right)^{2}-(1-k\eta_{1})\left((p_{j}+1)\delta b-\frac{1}{2}p_{j}\delta b\right)\right)\log R\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq R^{-2-2a+o(1)}\exp\Bigg(\frac{1}{2}k\eta_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(p_{j}+2)\delta b\log R\Bigg).

By (
8.12
),

\log\frac{R}{2|z-w|}\geq(k-1)\eta_{1}\log R-\log\log R=(1+o(1))\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)k\eta_{1}\log R.

So we have obtained that (
8.13
) is at most

R^{-2-2a+o(1)}\left(\frac{R}{|z-w|}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(p_{j}+2)\delta b}.

Since
p_{1},p_{2}\leq 1/\delta-1
, the exponent
\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{k})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(p_{j}+2)\delta b
is bounded by
(1-\frac{1}{k_{0}})^{-1}(1+\delta)b
which can be made arbitrarily close to
(1-\frac{1}{k_{0}})^{-1}b
by choosing
\delta
small enough. Recall further that
k_{0}=k_{0}(\eta_{1},\eta_{3})
can be chosen arbitrarily large by taking
\eta_{1}
small enough for any given
\eta_{3}>0
.
Hence the exponent
\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{k_{0}})^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(p_{j}+2)\delta b
can be made as close to
a
as desired. This concludes the proof.
∎

9
Occupation measure of branching Brownian motion (proof of Theorem
1.4
)

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.4
, which concerns the probability that a small ball has a large occupation measure for branching Brownian motion. We start with the lower bound, which follows quickly from Theorem
1.3
:

Proof of Theorem
1.4
– Lower bound.

Let
x_{0}>0
,
\varepsilon>0
. By Theorem
1.3
, there exists
x_{*}>0
and
R_{0}>0
such that for all
R\geq R_{0}
,
r\leq e^{-x_{*}}R
,
a>0
,

\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}\geq\frac{a}{2}r^{2}(\log R/r)^{2}|N_{r}>0\right)\geq(R/r)^{-(1+\varepsilon)a}.

The local time of
B(r)
can be decomposed as
\sum_{i=1\dots N_{r}}L_{i}
where
L_{i}
is the occupation measure accumulated by the progeny of the
i
-th pioneer of
\partial B(r)
. The
L_{i}
’s are independent of
N_{r}
and are i.i.d. and distributed according to the occupation measure of
B(r)
under
\mathbb{P}_{z,R}
where
z
is any point of
\partial B(r)
. Letting
n=\frac{(1+\varepsilon)^{2}a}{2}r^{2}(\log R/r)^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(L(B(r))\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}|N_{r}>0\right)\geq(R/r)^{-(1+\varepsilon)^{3}a}\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\right).

Recall that since the branching mechanism is critical,
\mathbb{E}\left[L_{i}\right]
is equal to the expected value of the local time in
B(r)
of a single Brownian motion that starts on
\partial B(r)
and that is killed on
\partial B(R)
. If
x_{*}
is large enough, this expectation is close to the one in infinite volume, more precisely it is larger than
(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}m_{1}r^{2}
(recall the definition (
1.6
) of
m_{1}
).
The probability on the right hand side of the above display is therefore at least

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\geq(1+\varepsilon)^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[L_{1}\right]n\right).

Because the
L_{i}
’s are bounded from below by 0, this probability is extremely close to 1.
This is for instance a consequence of standard large deviations for i.i.d. nonnegative random variables, sometimes called Benett’s inequality (see e.g.
[
BLM13
, Theorem 2.9]
) or
[
Dur19
, Theorem (9.5)]
).
This concludes the proof.
∎

We now move on to the proof of the upper bound (
1.8
) in Theorem
1.4
. We first explain the difficulty of its proof. Let
r\geq 1
.
As in the proof of the lower bound, we can decompose the occupation measure of the ball
B(r)
as
\sum_{i=1\dots N_{r}}L_{i}
where
L_{i}
is the occupation measure accumulated by the progeny of the
i
-th pioneer of
\partial B(r)
.
We want to argue that, given
N_{r}
, the sum
\sum_{i=1\dots N_{r}}L_{i}
is concentrated around
\mathbb{E}\left[L_{1}\right]N_{r}
. In the lower bound, this step was straightforward and we only used the nonnegativity of the
L_{i}
’s. For the more subtle upper bound, we need to guarantee a certain decay of their upper tail, which is the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 9.1
.

There exists
\lambda_{0}>0
such that for all
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{0}]
,

\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda}{r^{4}\log(R/r)}L(B(r))\right)\right]=1+\frac{\lambda m_{1}+o(1)}{r^{2}\log(R/r)}

(9.1)

where
o(1)\to 0
as
r/R\to 0
and
R\to\infty
.

Proof.

We first claim that there exists
\lambda_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that for all
\lambda\in[0,2\lambda_{0}]
,
R
large enough and
R/r
large enough,

\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda}{r^{4}\log(R/r)}L(B(r))\right)\right]\leq 1+\frac{C}{r^{2}\log(R/r)}.

(9.2)

This is a reformulation of a result obtained in
[
AS22
]
in the context of branching random walk; see in particular (9.2) therein.
Their proof is based on the following estimate which is the content of
[
AS22
, Proposition 9.1]
: there exists
c,C>0
such that for all
r\geq 1
and
t>r^{4}
,

\sup_{x\in B(2r)}\mathbb{P}_{x,2r}\left(L(B(2r))>t\right)\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}}\exp\left(-ct/r^{4}\right).

(9.3)

To get (
9.2
) from this estimate, Asselah and Schapira decompose
L(B(r))
as a sum over successive “waves” (or excursions) between
\partial B(r)
and
\partial B(2r)
of the occupation measure of
B(r)
accumulated during the wave. Each such elementary occupation measure is handled thanks to (
9.3
) wheareas the total number of terms involved in the sum is handled thanks to their knowledge on the number of pioneers. See
[
AS22
, Section 9]
for more details. The exact same approach can be done in the continuum (in fact more precise estimates on these pioneers were already obtained here in the previous sections). This leads to (
9.2
); we omit the details.

We will need in addition the following moment estimate:
for all
p\geq 1
, there exists
C_{p}>0
such that for all
r\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}_{r,\infty}\left[L(B(r))^{p}\right]\leq C_{p}r^{-2+4p}.

(9.4)

This type of statement is relatively to prove by induction on
p\geq 1
. As already explained, by criticality of the branching mechanism, the first moment coincides with the first moment of the local time of a single Brownian motion, which is equal to
m_{1}r^{2}
. For the second moment, one needs to handle the local times generated by two particles. One can decompose over the generation of the most recent common ancestor. The remaining trajectories are independent and the second moment factorises into the square of the first moment. Integrating out the randomness of the most recent common ancestor, one gets (
9.4
) for
p=2
. This procedure can be iterated to control moments of arbitrary order
p\geq 1
by considering the first branching event, leading to a diagrammatic sum representing the possible genealogical structure of the
p
particles. It has been carried out in great detail in the context of branching random walk in
[
AHJ21
, Section 3]
. We emphasise that we do not require any control on the growth of the constants
C_{p}
, which would require a careful analysis of these diagrammatic sums as was done in
[
AHJ21
]
. Here we will actually only use (
9.4
) with
p=2
and
4
, and thus omit the details.

We now explain how we obtain (
9.1
). Let us denote by
X=L(B(r))/(r^{4}\log(R/r))
. Let
\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{0}]
. Using the mean value theorem we can bound

\displaystyle 0\leq\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[e^{\lambda X}-1-\lambda X\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[e^{\lambda_{0}X}X^{2}\right]=\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[X^{2}\right]+\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[(e^{\lambda_{0}X}-1)X^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[X^{2}\right]+\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[(e^{\lambda_{0}X}-1)^{2}\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[X^{4}\right]^{1/2}

where we used Cauchy–Schwarz in the last inequality. By (
9.4
),

\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[X^{2}\right]\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}(\log(R/r))^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[X^{4}\right]^{1/2}\leq\frac{C}{r(\log(R/r))^{2}}

and
by (
9.2
),

\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[(e^{\lambda_{0}X}-1)^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[e^{2\lambda_{0}X}\right]-1\leq\frac{C\lambda_{0}^{2}}{r^{2}\log(R/r)}.

Thus (we absorb the dependence on
\lambda_{0}
into the constant
C
),

0\leq\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[e^{\lambda X}-1-\lambda X\right]\leq\frac{C}{r^{2}(\log(R/r))^{2}}.

Since
\mathbb{E}_{r,R}\left[L(B(r))\right]=(1+o(1))m_{1}r^{2}
, this concludes the proof of (
9.1
).
∎

Using directly the estimate (
9.1
) as well as Theorem
6.1
, we find that for all
\lambda<\min(\lambda_{0},2/m_{1})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(L(B(r))\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\right)\leq R^{-2+o(1)}(R/r)^{-\frac{am_{1}\lambda}{2}}.

If we were allowed to choose
\lambda
as close to
2/m_{1}
as possible, this would already imply the upper bound of Theorem
1.4
(without any restriction on
a\in(0,a_{0})
). However, we have no control over the
\lambda_{0}
in (
9.1
) at this stage, and hence we will need to proceed in a more complicated way, using once more the notion of “non-backtracking” particles from Section
8
.

Remark 9.2
.

In fact, we believe that the optimal value for
\lambda_{0}
in (
9.1
) is equal to
2/m_{1}
. However, it seems unlikely that the approach of
[
AS22
]
can directly achieve this level of precision.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
– Upper bound.

In this proof, we will assume that
a<\min(2,\lambda_{0}m_{1})
where
\lambda_{0}>0
is the constant appearing in (
9.1
).
Let
x_{0}>0
,
k_{\max}\geq 1
be a large integer and
\eta_{1}=1/(k_{\max}+1)
. Let
\eta_{2}>0
(inverse of a large integer) and
\varepsilon>0
be both much smaller than
\eta_{1}
.
Let
R_{0}>0
and
x_{*}>0
be large enough so that Theorems
1.3
and
6.1
and the estimate (
9.1
) apply with errors at most
\varepsilon
. Let
R>R_{0}^{1/\eta_{1}}
and
r\in[1,e^{-x_{*}/\eta_{1}}R]
.

For all
k=0,\dots,k_{\max}+1
, let
r_{k}=r(R/r)^{k\eta_{1}}
. By definition,
r_{0}=r
and
r_{k_{\max}+1}=R
. We have tuned the parameters to ensure that
r_{1}\geq R_{0}
and
r_{k+1}/r_{k}\geq e^{x_{*}}
for all
k
.
For each
k\in\{1,\dots,k_{\max}\}
, we let
\mathbf{P}_{r_{k}}
be the set of pioneers on
\partial B(r_{k})
emanating from the pioneers on
\partial B(r)
. By convention, we also set
\mathbf{P}_{r}
to be the set of pioneers on
\partial B(r)
.
For each
k\in\{0,\dots,k_{\max}\}
, let
\mathbf{L}_{k}
be the local time in
B(r)
induced by
\mathbf{P}_{r_{k}}
where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{k+1})
. In words,
\mathbf{L}_{k}
captures the local time in
B(r)
of particles that backtracked exactly to level
r_{k}
.
By construction, the total local time of
B(r)
is equal to

\sum_{k=0}^{k_{\max}}\mathbf{L}_{k}.

Let
(\mathcal{F}_{k})_{k=0,\dots,k_{\max}}
be the filtration:

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma(\#\mathbf{P}_{r_{\ell}},\ell=0,\dots,k),\quad k=0,\dots,k_{\max}.

(9.5)

Let
k\in\{1,\dots,k_{\max}\}
. Note that conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
(\mathbf{L}_{k},\mathbf{L}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{L}_{k_{\max}})
is independent from
(\mathbf{L}_{0},\mathbf{L}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{L}_{k-1})
and is distributed as follows.
Consider a branching Brownian motion with
\#\mathbf{P}_{r_{k}}
initial particles located on
\partial B(r_{k})
. Let
\ell_{k}
be the total local time of
B(r)
where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{k+1})
. For
i=1,\dots,k_{\max}-k
, let
\ell_{k+i}
be the local time of
B(r)
generated by the pioneers of
\partial B(r_{k+i})
where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{k+i+1})
. Then the law of
(\mathbf{L}_{k},\mathbf{L}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{L}_{k_{\max}})
conditionally given
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is the same as the law of
(\ell_{k},\dots,\ell_{k_{\max}})
.

For each
\mathbf{k}\in\{0,\dots,k_{\max}\}
, we define the events

E_{\mathbf{k}}:=\bigl\{\max\{k=0,\dots,k_{\max}:\#\mathbf{P}_{r_{k}}\geq 1\}=\mathbf{k}\bigr\},\quad F_{\mathbf{k}}:=\biggl\{\forall k=0,\dots,\mathbf{k},\#\mathbf{P}_{r_{k}}\leq r_{k}^{2}\frac{(\log R/r)^{2}}{\log\log R/r}\biggr\}.

Note that
F_{\mathbf{k}}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbf{k}}
. By Lemma
2.7
, the probability

\mathbb{P}\left(F_{k_{\max}}^{c}|N_{r}>0\right)
decays faster than any polynomial in
R/r
(here we use that
\sup_{R}s_{*}(R)<0
).
In the rest of the proof, we will work on the event
F_{k_{\max}}\cap\{N_{r}>0\}
.
We first keep track of the contribution of
\mathbf{L}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{k_{\max}}\mathbf{L}_{k}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2},F_{k_{\max}}\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{L}_{0}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\right)

(9.6)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum_{p=0}^{1/\eta_{2}-1}\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathbf{L}_{0}\in[p,p+1)\frac{am_{1}\eta_{2}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2},\sum_{k=1}^{k_{\max}}\mathbf{L}_{k}\geq(1-(p+1)\eta_{2})\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2},F_{k_{\max}}\Big).

We first claim that the killing on
\partial B(r_{1})
and the estimate (
9.1
) are enough to show that (recall that
\varepsilon
and
\eta_{2}
are much smaller than
\eta_{1}
so that
\varepsilon^{\prime}
defined below is small)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{L}_{0}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\,\Big|\,N_{r}>0\right)\leq(R/r)^{-a+\varepsilon^{\prime}}\quad\text{where}\quad\varepsilon^{\prime}=m_{1}\lambda_{0}\frac{\varepsilon+\eta_{2}}{2\eta_{1}}+\varepsilon.

(9.7)

Indeed, we decompose
\mathbf{L}_{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{r}}L_{i}
where
L_{i}
,
i\geq 1
, are i.i.d. and distributed as the local time of
B(r)
starting with one particle on
\partial B(r)
and where particles are killed on
\partial B(r_{1})
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{L}_{0}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\,\Big|\,N_{r}>0\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}\geq\frac{a}{2}r^{2}(\log R/r)^{2}\,\Big|\,N_{r}>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt+\sum_{p=0}^{1/\eta_{2}-1}\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}\in[n_{p},n_{p+1})\,\Big|\,N_{r}>0\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_{p+1}}L_{i}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\right)

(9.8)

where
n_{p}=p\eta_{2}\frac{a}{2}r^{2}(\log R/r)^{2}
,
p\geq 0
. By Markov’s inequality and (
9.1
), the last probability in the above display is at most

\displaystyle(R/r)^{-\frac{am_{1}\lambda_{0}}{2\eta_{1}}}\mathbb{E}_{r,r_{1}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{\lambda_{0}}{r^{4}(\log r_{1}/r)}L(B(r))\right)\right]^{n_{p+1}}

\displaystyle=(R/r)^{-\frac{am_{1}\lambda_{0}}{2\eta_{1}}}\left(1+\frac{m_{1}\lambda_{0}}{r^{2}\eta_{1}(\log R/r)}(1+\varepsilon)\right)^{n_{p+1}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{am_{1}\lambda_{0}}{2\eta_{1}}(1-(p+1)\eta_{2}(1+\varepsilon))\log(R/r)\right).

Moreover, by Theorem
1.3
,
\mathbb{P}_{e^{-x_{0}}R,R}\left(N_{r}\geq n_{p}|N_{r}>0\right)\leq(R/r)^{-ap\eta_{2}+\varepsilon}
.
This shows that the sum in (
9.8
) is at most

\displaystyle(R/r)^{-a+o(1)}\sum_{p=0}^{1/\eta_{2}-1}\exp\left(\left((1-p\eta_{2})a(1-\frac{m_{1}\lambda_{0}}{2\eta_{1}})+m_{1}\lambda_{0}\frac{\varepsilon(p+1)\eta_{2}+\eta_{2}}{2\eta_{1}}+\varepsilon\right)\log\frac{R}{r}\right).

We simply bound
1-\frac{m_{1}\lambda_{0}}{2\eta_{1}}\leq 0
and
(p+1)\eta_{2}\leq 1
to get that the sum in (
9.8
) is at most
(R/r)^{-a+\varepsilon^{\prime}+o(1)}
where
\varepsilon^{\prime}
is defined in (
9.7
).
Together with Theorem
1.3
this establishes the claim (
9.7
), which handles the first term on the right hand side of (
9.6
).

It remains to bound the sum over
p
appearing on the right hand side of (
9.6
). By using that
\mathbf{L}_{0}
and
(\mathbf{L}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{L}_{k_{\max}})
are independent conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, we can further bound the left hand side of (
9.6
) by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{L}_{0}\geq\frac{am_{1}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{p=0}^{1/\eta_{2}-1}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbf{L}_{0}\in[p,p+1)\frac{am_{1}\eta_{2}}{2}r^{4}(\log R/r)^{2}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{F}_{1}\right)\times

(9.9)
